...concerns various entities. It is especially evident to the wise that this holds true in the absolute knowledge of our **chemistry** <term>chemistry<note>original: chymicæ; in this 17th-century context, "chemistry" and "alchemy" were often used interchangeably to describe the study of natural and spiritual transformations.</note></term>. For the wise, it is quite easy to grow rich, if they only wish to do so. Yet for the foolish—who desire nothing more than wealth—it is most difficult. 

No one of sound mind will ever admit that the rewards of **Wisdom** <note>original: Sapientiæ</note> are riches themselves, for wealth is bound up with a strange variety of hardships and is exposed to the mockery of fortune. Therefore, riches are not even to be desired. 

Since these things are so, the lack of restraint in men and their lack of wisdom, combined with the stubbornness of their passions, drive many headlong. As **Sophocles** <note>A famous ancient Greek playwright known for his tragedies.</note> once said, they are like the "raging horses of desire." They value this miserable and precarious life and its comforts more than almost any path to true happiness. For such people, it is inevitable that before they even begin to become wise, extreme misfortune and **Ate** <term>Ate<note>original Greek: Ἄτη; in Greek mythology, the personification of ruin, delusion, and the blind folly that leads humans to their downfall.</note></term> herself will
->cast them down and destroy them.<-

->THE END.<-

# THE PREFACE OF AGOSTINO
## NIFO, PHYSICIAN AND PHILOSOPHER
### of Sessa, to his books on Auguries, addressed to the illustrious and magnanimous Count Guido Rangoni.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'Q' containing an ornate rose or floral motif within a square frame.</image-desc>
It is not difficult to understand, magnanimous Guido, how much vanity <note>The author uses "vanity" here to mean empty or groundless beliefs, a common critique of pagan practices by Renaissance scholars</note> flourished among the men of antiquity, based on those things they handed down concerning auguries <term>Augury: the ancient practice of interpreting the will of the gods through the flight, song, or behavior of birds</term> and the arts of divining. The peoples of Cilicia and Pisidia, as well as their neighbor Pamphylia <note>These were regions in ancient Asia Minor (modern-day Turkey)</note>, believed that future events were revealed by the flights and songs of birds. Democritus, Chrysippus, Diogenes of Babylon, Antipater, and Posidonius <note>These represent a "who’s who" of ancient Greek philosophy, particularly the Stoics, who often defended divination as a logical part of a providential universe</note> all approved of this practice. Romulus, the greatest of augurs, founded the City <note>Rome</note> only after taking the auspices. The ancient Kings and the Romans themselves placed such great faith in auguries that they would conduct no business at all—public or private—without first taking the auspices; they even claimed that their temples were "august" <note>The word *augustus* (consecrated/holy) shares a linguistic root with *augury*</note> and that these same auguries were, from the very beginning, granted and increased by Jupiter’s own hand. Tiberius Gracchus

<summary>In this preface, Agostino Nifo introduces his study of augury by recounting how ancient civilizations and famous philosophers once held a deep, almost absolute faith in the prophetic power of birds.</summary>
<keywords>Agostino Nifo, Guido Rangoni, augury, divination, Roman history, Stoicism, Romulus, auspices</keywords>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the preface by Agostino Nifo, which began on page 3. The sentence starting at the top of this page likely completes a thought from the previous page concerning historical figures who prioritized certain types of divination.</meta>

->DE AVGVRII S<-

...placed the discipline of the **augurs** and **haruspices** <term>Haruspices: ancient diviners who interpreted the will of the gods by inspecting the entrails of sacrificed animals</term> before all other forms of divination; this discipline flourished in Telmessus, a city of Caria <note>Telmessus was a famous center for divination in Asia Minor, now Fethiye, Turkey</note>. The **Phrygians** and **Arabs** also obeyed the signs of birds, as did the **Umbrians** <note>An ancient Italic people of the central Italian peninsula</note>. Furthermore, **Pliny**, in his books on *Natural History* <note>Referring to Pliny the Elder’s encyclopedic Naturalis Historia</note>, not only grants credit to these things but also proves them with many examples. **Livy** <note>Titus Livius, the Roman historian</note> likewise held auguries in high esteem everywhere, and noted them whenever it was required. **Hesiod**, in his account of the siege of Nineveh <note>original: "obsessionis Nini"</note>, depicts an eagle—the ruler of augury—drinking. **Aristotle**, the master of all philosophers, not only places faith in auguries but also advises that much can be gathered from the study of animals. Hence, in his books on the *History of Animals*, he says: "Even when a hen is cut open, in the place where the eggs are attached to the females, things have been found—yellow in color and the size of a full egg—which the augurs take as a portent <term>Portent (ostentum): an unusual or miraculous phenomenon interpreted as a sign of future events</term>." Moreover, when he was speaking about milk and colostrum <note>The first milk produced by mammals immediately after giving birth</note>, he remarked: "But they consider that these things should rather be numbered among portents; for to that master of livestock from Lemnos <note>An island in the northern Aegean Sea</note> who sought counsel, the god replied that there would be a greater increase in his wealth <note>original: "peculii," referring to personal property or cattle</note>."

### BOOK I.
a 3

And when he spoke of **sneezing** <note>original: "de sternutamento"; in the ancient world, a sneeze was often interpreted as a divine sign or a momentary release of the spirit</note>, he said it is a sign of augury and one of the types of all spirits, holy and sacred, through which he seems not only to give credit to **auguries** but even to prove them. 

Indeed, there were very many most famous **augurs** who both granted faith to the **auspices** <term>Auspices: the practice of observing the flight of birds to determine the approval of the gods</term> and observed the auguries. Among these was **Deiotarus**, King of Galatia <note>A contemporary of Julius Caesar and Cicero; the latter defended him in a famous speech</note>, who, as **Cicero** says, never did anything except by taking the auspices first. **Tisameus**, the son of Antiochus, was so skilled in the art of augury that he delivered oracles at **Delphi** <note>The most important oracle site in the Greek world, dedicated to Apollo</note>. 

**Leucadius**, a most famous **haruspex** <term>Haruspex: a priest who interpreted the divine will by inspecting the entrails of sacrificial animals</term>, who was known by the name **Hyppomachus**, observed auguries among the Greeks. **Vestius** was most noble in the discipline of augury; for he, as **Varro** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, a prolific Roman scholar and writer</note> writes in his *Books of Antiquities*, asserted that the designated end of the Roman Empire would come after 1,200 years <note>This prophecy was based on the twelve vultures seen by Romulus at the founding of Rome, each representing a century</note>. 

**Theocritus** was an outstanding augur, who surpassed all the augurs of his time in the authority of his divination. **Umbritius** attributed such faith to auguries that he asserted they were the very words of the gods themselves. **Tyburnus**, the son of Am- <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword indicates the name is Amphiaraus, a legendary seer of Greek myth</note>

->### PREFACE.<-

...thority of **Amphiaraus**. 

The son of **Amphiaraus** <note>Amphiaraus was a legendary seer; his son, often identified as Tiburnus, was said to have founded the city of Tibur</note> was so devoted to auguries that the people of **Tibur** <note>Modern Tivoli, near Rome</note> did not wish to be called by any name other than that derived from Tiburnus. Amphiaraus, the son of Oecles, was a Greek augur; when King **Adrastus** wanted to lead him into the war against **Thebes**, he refused, and to avoid being forced, he hid himself, because he foresaw that he would die there. **Tiresias**, King of the Thebans <note>The famous blind prophet of Greek mythology, featured in the stories of Oedipus and Odysseus</note>, not only placed his faith in auguries, but it is also recorded that he was the inventor of the art of augury. 

**Tages**, however, was the first of all to provide the discipline of **auspices** <term>Auspices: divine signs taken from the behavior of birds</term> <note>In Etruscan mythology, Tages was a miraculous child who rose from a plowed field to teach the people the art of divination</note>. These are the men who clung to the vanities of auguries. Therefore, so that these ancient figures do not provide an excuse for the unlearned <note>original: "prophanis", referring to those outside the sacred or educated circle</note> to fall into error, we have decided that two books on auguries must be composed. 

The first book will describe all the vanities that these men handed down. In the second book, however, we will refute the opinions and reasons by which they granted credit to such vanities; we will also explain which auguries should be trusted, and to what extent one should practice divination. Truly, when a work is published, it is customary that it be dedicated to a distinguished man, not

ON AUGURIES

### WHAT IS TO BE UNDERSTOOD BY THE NAME AUGURY, and in what order we should proceed.

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring foliage and a stylized floral background.</image-desc>Before we come to the subject that brings us to the topic of augury, it seems necessary to explain what the ancients understood by the term "augury," and how we intend to use it in our discussion.

Augury, then, is a certain type of **divination** <term>Divination: the practice of seeking knowledge of the future or the unknown by supernatural means</term> which is gathered from the observation of birds themselves.

<margin>Aristotle</margin>Divination, however, as **Aristotle** <note>The author refers here to Aristotle’s work *On Divination in Sleep* (De Divinatione per Somnum)</note> says in his book on dreams, is spoken of in two ways: it is either **natural** or **artificial**.

**Natural divination** <term>Natural divination: omens that occur spontaneously in nature, perceived as direct messages from the divine or nature without requiring human ritual</term> occurs when a bird, through its flight, its cries, or even its eating habits, unexpectedly predicts some future good or evil by its own nature. It is of this natural divination that **Virgil** <note>Publius Vergilius Maro (70–19 BCE), the Roman poet whose works were often cited as authorities in the Middle Ages and Renaissance</note> speaks when he says:

<margin>Virgil</margin>> *And had not a raven, from a hollow oak tree on the left, previously warned me to cut short these new disputes by any means, neither your Moeris here, nor Menalcas himself, would be alive.* <note>original Latin: "Quod nisi me quacunq; nouas incidere lites, / Ante sinistra caua monuisset ab ilice cornix. / Nec tuus hic Mœris, nec uiueret ipse Menalcas." This is a quote from Virgil's *Eclogues* IX, lines 14–16.</note>

<meta>catchword: Artificial,</meta>

<summary>This page defines augury as a form of divination derived from birds and distinguishes between 'natural' and 'artificial' divination, citing Aristotle and Virgil as authorities.</summary>
<keywords>augury, divination, Aristotle, Virgil, natural divination, omens, birds, Eclogues</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical definitions of divination, moving from "natural" divination (covered at the end of the previous page) to "artificial" divination, which requires a trained practitioner or interpreter.</meta>

# BOOK I.

**Artificial divination**, <term>Artificial divination: also known as "learned" divination; omens that require a trained specialist to interpret according to a set of rules or traditions, rather than being a direct, spontaneous message from nature</term> on the other hand, is the interpretation performed by an **augur** <term>Augur: a Roman official whose job was to interpret the will of the gods by observing the behavior of birds</term>. In this practice, the specialist interprets natural signs to find their specific meaning. For this reason, **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and orator whose works on law and religion were foundational for Renaissance scholars</note> in his book *On the Laws*, says that there are two kinds of priests: one kind that presides over ceremonies and sacred rites, and another that interprets the utterances of fate-speakers and prophets, which are otherwise unknown. <note>original Latin: "unum, quod præsit ceremoniis & sacris, alterum quod interpretatur fatidicorum & uatum effata incognitá q̄;"</note>

Divination involving birds was divided into three types. For some birds predicted the future by their **flight**, others by their **song**, and others by their **eating**. <note>original Latin: "aliæ uolatu, aliæ cātu, aliæ gustatu"</note> For example, the sacred chickens <note>pulli: the sacred chickens kept by Roman armies to provide omens before battle</note> predicted the unhappy conflict at **Numantia** <note>Numantia: a city in ancient Spain famous for its long resistance against Rome</note> that was inflicted upon **Mancinus**; <note>Gaius Hostilius Mancinus, a Roman consul who suffered a humiliating defeat in 137 BCE after ignoring bad omens</note> the birds refused to accept their food and instead flew out of their cage.

<margin>
How **Auspice**
differs from
**Augury**.
</margin>
**Auspice**, however, differs from augury, since augury properly pertains only to the chattering and voice of birds, while auspice is derived from the flight of birds. Yet **Nonius Marcellus** <note>A 4th-century Roman grammarian and lexicographer</note> teaches that Auspice is the inspection of birds, while Augury is the interpretation of all things by conjecture.

<margin>
**Haruspicy**.
</margin>
**Haruspicy** <term>Haruspicy: the practice of divining the future by inspecting the internal organs (entrails) of sacrificed animals</term> was performed by inspecting the victims on the altar.

Those who performed this were called **Extispices**, <term>Extispices: from 'exta' (entrails) and 'specio' (to look at)</term> named from the practice of inspecting the entrails.

-># ON AUGURIES<-

## WHAT IS TO BE UNDERSTOOD BY THE NAME AUGURY,
## and in what order we should proceed.

Before we arrive at the subject that brings us to the study of augury, it seems necessary to explain what the ancients understood by "augury," and in what sense we are using the term in this discussion.

Augury is, therefore, a certain kind of divination <term>Divination: the practice of seeking knowledge of the future or the unknown by supernatural means</term> which is gathered from the birds themselves.

Divination, moreover, as **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE), the Greek philosopher whose works on natural science were standard textbooks for centuries; the reference here is to his treatise *On Divination through Sleep*</note> says in his book *On Dreams*, is described in two ways: either as **natural** or **artificial**.

Natural divination is that which comes from the bird itself, which—by its flight, its song, or its appetite—predicts something of future good or evil for us unexpectedly by its very nature. It is of this natural divination that **Virgil** <note>Publius Vergilius Maro (70–19 BCE), the Roman poet. This quote is from his *Eclogues* (9.14–16), where the characters Moeris and Menalcas discuss how a bird's omen saved them from a violent dispute.</note> speaks when he says:

> *Unless a crow, appearing on the left from a hollow oak, had previously warned me to cut short the new disputes in any way possible, neither your Moeris nor Menalcas himself would be alive.* <note>original Latin: "Quod nisi me quacunq; nouas incidere lites, Ante sinistra caua monuisset ab ilice cornix. Nec tuus hic Mœris, nec uiueret ipse Menalcas."</note>

<note>Vocabulary list from the original page: augury, divination, Aristotle, natural, artificial, Virgil, crow, Moeris, Menalcas</note>

<summary>This page defines augury as a branch of divination and introduces Aristotle's distinction between natural and artificial omens. It uses a famous passage from Virgil's poetry to illustrate "natural" divination as a spontaneous warning from a bird.</summary>
<keywords>augury, divination, Aristotle, natural divination, artificial divination, Virgil, omens, crows, classical philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues from the definitions established on page 8, transitioning from "natural" divination (signs occurring without human invitation) to "artificial" divination (the expert interpretation of signs). It provides classical authorities to distinguish between various types of omens and the specific roles of Roman priests.</meta>

-># BOOK I.<-

<sig>a 5</sig>

**Artificial** [divination], however, is the interpretation of the **augur** <term>Augur: a Roman official who observed and interpreted the signs of birds and other natural phenomena to determine the will of the gods</term>, by which he interprets natural divination into its specific meaning. Hence **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and philosopher. This reference is to his work *On the Laws* (De Legibus, 2.8.20), where he discusses the religious foundations of the state.</note> says in his book *On the Laws* that there are two kinds of priests: one that presides over ceremonies and sacred rites, and another that interprets the strange and unknown utterances of soothsayers and seers. There were three types of divination involving birds. For some predicted the future by their flight, others by their song, and others by their appetite. Indeed, the sacred chickens predicted the disastrous conflict at **Numantia** <note>The Numantine War (143–133 BCE) in modern Spain. The Roman consul Gaius Hostilius Mancinus was forced to surrender in 137 BCE, a disgrace later attributed to his disregard for these bird omens.</note> inflicted upon **Mancinus**, for they would not take their food but instead flew out of their cage.

<margin>How an auspice differs from an augury.</margin>
An **auspice** actually differs from an **augury**, since an augury is properly performed by the chattering or voice of birds alone, while an auspice is from the flight of birds. **Nonius Marcellus** <note>Nonius Marcellus (early 4th century CE), a Latin grammarian whose work *De compendiosa doctrina* is a major source for early Latin vocabulary and fragments of lost literature.</note>, however, teaches that an auspice is the inspection of birds, while an augury is an interpretation drawn from all things.

<margin>Haruspicy.</margin>
**Haruspicy** <term>Haruspicy: the practice of inspecting the internal organs of sacrificed animals to seek divine guidance</term> was performed by inspecting the victims upon the altar.
**Extispices**, moreover, were so called from the inspection of the **entrails** <term>Exta: the major internal organs of a sacrificial animal, specifically the heart, lungs, and liver</term>.

<meta>This page continues the classification of divinatory signs, moving from the technical distinction between "auspice" and "augury" on page 9 to the specific definitions of "omen," "presage," "vaticination," "ostent," and "portent." It establishes the vocabulary used by classical authors like Livy to categorize supernatural occurrences.</meta>

-># ON THE SIGNS OF AUGURY<-

<meta>catchword: men se</meta>

<margin>
Omen.
<unclear>augury</unclear>
</margin>
An **omen** is the focused intention of the person taking the auspices, by which the observer accepts something that occurs to them as an augury. It is called both a "good" and a "bad" omen, as is read more than once in the works of **Livy** <note>Titus Livius (64 BCE–17 CE), the Roman historian who chronicled the history of Rome and its religious traditions.</note>. For example, when **Ulysses** <note>The Greek hero Odysseus, returning home to Ithaca in Homer's Odyssey.</note> was plotting the death of the suitors, he prayed to **Jupiter** <note>The king of the gods; in the Greek original, this was Zeus.</note> that he would give him a double sign, so that he might know for certain that what he had conceived in his mind would be accomplished. To him, Jupiter immediately thundered; then, he heard a girl who had been brought to the grinding mill in the house praying to the gods that the suitors might not eat in the house of Ulysses for even one day longer. Ulysses accepted this as an omen, and the outcome of the event was not lacking: for on that day, the suitors were killed to a man by Ulysses and **Telemachus**.

<margin>Presage.</margin>
A **presage**, however, is more common. For every animal that "presages" anything that is presented to it is said to presage; that is, to feel or perceive future events beforehand.

<margin>Vaticination.</margin>
**Vaticination** <term>Vaticination: the act of prophesying or predicting future events through divine inspiration, often associated with the inspired speech of a seer</term> is divination itself, which, as we have said, is either natural or artificial.

<margin>Ostent.</margin>
An **ostent** <term>Ostent: from the Latin 'ostendere' (to show), a sign or wonder that reveals something about the future immediately</term> is that which shows anything of the future immediately, such as **comets**, thunder, a monster, and things of that kind.

<margin>Portent.</margin>
A **portent** <term>Portent: a miraculous sign, often one that seems to deviate from the natural order</term> is something which, although it may be natural, yet rarely...

<summary>This page defines and distinguishes between various types of divinatory signs—omen, presage, vaticination, ostent, and portent—using the story of Ulysses and the suitors as a primary example of an omen.</summary>
<keywords>omen, presage, vaticination, ostent, portent, Ulysses, Jupiter, Livy, divination, Roman religion</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page completes the definitions of divinatory terms started on the previous page, finishing the description of a "portent" and moving into "prodigy." It then establishes that the author will use "augury" as an umbrella term for all these signs, beginning a specific taxonomy of signs starting with sneezing.</meta>

# BOOK I.

<meta>catchword: portent- (from previous page) / -um (on this page)</meta>

[A portent] <note>original: "portentum", completing the word from the previous page.</note> shows itself, or rather it announces something in the future that will occur after an interval of time.

<margin>Prodigy.</margin>
A **prodigy** is said to be something that happens according to nature and occurs frequently, yet it always foretells something evil. We see this in **Homer’s** <note>The legendary Greek epic poet, author of the Iliad and Odyssey.</note> *Odyssey*, Book 21. For when **Ulysses** <note>The Roman name for Odysseus, the hero of the Odyssey.</note>, contrary to the expectations of all the suitors, had strung his bow with no effort—the very bow with which he was about to finish them off—**Jupiter** <note>The king of the gods, equivalent to the Greek Zeus.</note> thundered with a massive crash. This prodigy portended that Ulysses’ attempts against the suitors would not be in vain, as the outcome later proved. Furthermore, while the Greeks were struggling, Jupiter gave a prodigy of calamity by thundering throughout the entire night, as recorded in Homer's *Iliad*, Book 11. Likewise, when both the Greek and Trojan battle lines stood ready for combat, Jupiter, foreseeing a great slaughter of the Greeks, rained blood (*Iliad*, Book 11).

However, it pleases us to use the name of **augury** <term>Augury: originally the Roman practice of divining the will of the gods from the flight of birds, but used here as a general term for all types of divination.</term> for all these signs and for any others which the ancients interpreted as signifying the future. Therefore, in reviewing these matters, we shall speak of auguries: first, those which the ancients derived from **sneezing**; and then, concerning the auguries which come from...

# ON AUGURIES

[signs] are taken from the tremors <note>original: "tremoribus"</note> of each part of our body; and finally, concerning auguries which, received from any other sources whatsoever, they related to the signaling of the future. For the common people believed there to be auguries in all these matters.

But let us explain what things are necessary in auguries according to the practice of the ancients. For the vanity of the pagans <term>Pagans: original "gentilium," referring to the non-Christian peoples of antiquity, such as the Greeks and Romans.</term> believes that four things are necessary in the use of auguries.
<margin>1</margin> **The Object**, which occurs beyond the intention—that is, unexpectedly—to the one taking the auspices <term>Auspices: the practice of divining the future or the will of the gods by observing natural signs, such as the flight of birds.</term>, whether it be a bird, or a wild beast, or a sneeze, or a tremor, or any other thing.
<margin>2</margin> **The Person** taking the auspices, to whom such an object offers itself unexpectedly and beyond their intention.
<margin>3</margin> **The Intention** of the one taking the auspices, by which the object that 
<margin>4</margin> occurs is referred to as an augury. **And the Hour**, in which the object—which occurs unexpectedly and beyond intention, with no preceding reasoning—is accepted as an augury by a kind of natural
->impulse for an augury<-

# BOOK I.

<meta>Catchword: to move,</meta>

### Concerning the objects of augury.

Regarding the objects used in augury <note>original: "obiectis augurificis"</note>, there were various observations among the ancients. For some use sneezing as an augural object; others use tremors and the twitching <note>original: "saltibus," referring to involuntary muscle spasms or "leaps" of the pulse</note> of specific parts of the body. Others use humans, birds, and wild beasts as augural objects. Some use birds alone, while others use every single thing that occurs unexpectedly <note>original: "ex insperato"</note>. Finally, there are others who have reduced the objects of augury to twelve, according to the number of the twelve signs <term>Signs: The twelve constellations of the Zodiac through which the celestial bodies travel.</term> through which the Moon runs as it sets the auguries in motion. We shall speak of these later; for now, let it be enough to have touched upon these matters in a general way.

### Concerning the person taking the auspices.

Next, we must discuss the person taking the auspices <term>Auspices: The practice of observing signs, originally the flight of birds, to determine a course of action.</term>. For the mathematicians <term>Mathematicians: In this context, the term refers to "mathematici" or astrologers—scholars who calculated the positions of the stars to predict future events.</term> assert that it is not every person for whom auguries portend anything of evil or good. For this reason, some teach that the person for whom auguries predict the future is someone who holds a special status <note>original: "prærogotiuam," implying a person of high rank or specific destiny</note> among other men—and they are not moved to this conclusion without reason. For if the order of the universe, as they believe, sets auguries in motion, it does so only for the sake of human benefit,

<summary>This page categorizes the various objects used as omens—ranging from sneezes and muscle twitches to birds and the moon—and begins to argue that auguries are specifically directed toward significant individuals rather than the general population.</summary>
<keywords>augury, omens, sneezing, muscle tremors, zodiac signs, lunar movement, mathematicians, astrologers, status, cosmic order</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Person" involved in augury. The previous page ended while explaining that some experts (astrologers) believe omens only apply to specific individuals. This page completes that thought, suggesting that a leader's "intention" or natural focus makes them more susceptible to signs, before providing a counter-argument that even the humble can receive divine warnings.</meta>

->**CONCERNING AUGURIES**<-

...seems to move, and especially the intention of that man who stands above others, since he is more attuned <note>original: "intentus," meaning focused or naturally inclined toward a purpose</note> by nature itself. Furthermore, because the well-being or misfortune of many people depends upon the good or bad fortune of a preeminent man, they believe that auguries predict future events only for illustrious men—those who lead others through power, virtue, wealth, or dominion. 

However, we read the opposite. For we have found in the books of history that auguries have signaled something of good or evil even to lowly persons <note>original: "deiectis personis," referring to those of low social standing or the "dejected"</note>. Because of this, the vanity of the pagans <note>original: "gentilium vanitas," a common Renaissance term for non-Christian or classical superstitions</note> holds that every man for whom auguries occur can be a subject of them, provided that he accepts the augural objects as omens when they present themselves, as we shall discuss. 

Indeed, lowly persons sometimes excel even illustrious men in virtues and learning; for this reason, as Aristotle says in the tenth book of the *Ethics* <note>Referring to Aristotle’s *Nicomachean Ethics*, Book X, which discusses the contemplative life and divine favor.</note>, they are more beloved by nature itself and by God himself. Therefore, it follows that every person is suitable for these auguries to happen to them, provided that the augural objects occur unexpectedly <note>original: "ex insperato"</note>, beyond the reach of human reason or premeditation.

BOOK I.

### Concerning the intention of the one taking the auspices.

For this reason, unless the person taking the auspices <note>original: "persona auspicans," the individual observing or performing the ritual of divination</note> takes up the object that occurs unexpectedly as an omen through a certain natural impulse, it can in no way be an augury, as the augurs write. For insofar as we accept an object that appears by surprise as a sign through this natural instinct, it is determined that a star has moved both the object itself—so that it might cross our path—and our own mind—so that it might interpret the event as an omen. Indeed, all natural movements are regulated by celestial motion <note>The author is connecting astrology with divination, suggesting that the stars coordinate both the physical sign and the observer's psychological reaction to it.</note>. Thus, when an object meets us by a natural impulse, and we, through an impulse of nature, accept it as an augury, it happens that both of these impulses are governed by the movement of the heavens.

We have an example of this in Scipio Africanus. When he was disembarking from a small boat and fell to the ground, he immediately turned the accident into an augury, saying, "I hold you, Earth" <note>original: "Teneo te tellus." This is a famous historical anecdote where Scipio turned a potentially "bad" omen—falling on his face—into a "good" one by claiming he was embracing the land he intended to conquer.</note>. This would not have been a true augury had it not been transformed into one by a certain natural impulse from Scipio as he interpreted the sign.

Moreover, when the Macedonian War was assigned to Lucius Paullus <note>Lucius Aemilius Paullus Macedonicus (c. 229–160 BCE), a Roman consul who defeated King Perseus of Macedon.</note>...

<summary>This page discusses the psychological and astrological mechanics of divination, arguing that an event only becomes an "augury" when the observer's mind, guided by the stars, instinctively recognizes it as a sign. The author illustrates this with Scipio Africanus's famous ability to reinterpret a stumble as a sign of victory.</summary>

# ON AUGURIES

The Senate had issued an extraordinary decree <note>original: "senatus extra ordinem decrevisset," referring here to the special assignment of a military command</note>, and on that very day, he had returned home toward evening. As he was kissing his little daughter <margin>}</margin> Tertia, who was very small at the time, he noticed she was quite sad. When he asked her why she was so sad, she said, "Father, Persa is dead." Then, embracing the girl more tightly, he said, "I accept the omen, my little daughter" <note>original: "accipio omen." In Roman tradition, "accepting" an omen was a formal act that confirmed the sign's power.</note>. Now, it was a little dog by that name which the girl had as a pet that had died. But Lucius Paulus accepted the omen as a sign that Perses, the King of the Macedonians <note>original: "Persam Macedonum regem." The name of the dog, Persa, was identical to the name of the King, Perseus, whom Paulus was assigned to fight.</note>, against whom the command of the province had been assigned to him, would perish in that war—which indeed came to pass.

Furthermore, that the observer's intention is necessary in auguries is shown by the human head found in the Tarpeian sanctuary <note>The Tarpeian Hill, part of the Capitoline Hill in Rome. The discovery of a head (caput) there was said to give the "Capitol" its name.</note>. Seeking to transfer its destiny of power to Etruria, Olenus Calenus, a most famous seer, questioned the Roman envoys: "Did we find a head here?" This destiny would have been transferred to Etruria if the envoys, having been warned in advance by the seer’s own son, had not replied: "We do not say it was found precisely 'here' <note>meaning, in the seer's hands or in Etruria</note>, but we say the head was found in Rome." Let these examples suffice so that the powers of portents may become apparent.

->BOOK I.<-

...to be within our power, and that as each thing is accepted, so does it have force. Pliny writes this in Book 28, Chapter 2 <note>Pliny the Elder, *Naturalis Historia* 28.2. Pliny argues that the power of omens is subject to our own agency: they only have power if we acknowledge them.</note>. It is clear, therefore, that no object can be an omen-bearing sign for us unless we have taken a sign from it by a natural impulse <note>original: "naturali impetu." This refers to a spontaneous, instinctive reaction of the mind rather than a calculated decision.</note>.

### *On the chosen hour in which an omen-bearing*
### *object is recorded as an augury.*

And since astronomers teach how to find the true position of the star which is the causal factor by means of the "true time," let us speak of time. Some, therefore, assert that the true time is when the omen-bearing object is encountered by us unexpectedly. For at that time, the object is seen to be moved by the star which is the cause of it. Others, who are more skilled, judge the true time to be that moment in which the person who encounters it accepts the object as an augury by a natural impulse. Indeed, one must pay close attention to this utterance, which falls from the mouth of the man without reflection <note>original: "inconsulto," meaning unintentionally or without prior thought.</note> when the object is encountered. For, as Pliny says, this is what is first called an "omen" in augury, which the vanity of the heathens <note>original: "gentilium vanitas." The author uses this term to describe the beliefs of the ancient Romans and Greeks from a later perspective, likely to distance his study of the mechanics of signs from "pagan" worship.</note> believed to happen by divine favor. ### ON THE SCIENCE OF AUGURY ###
<meta>catchword: it is clear</meta>

...occurrences happen at the same moment of time, and for no other reason than that a celestial star powerfully moves the nature of both, and impels both simultaneously. From these things, it is possible to deduce that nothing can be omen-bearing <term>augurificum: a technical term for an object or event that carries the weight of a divine sign or prophecy</term> for us, unless we, from that certain natural impulse, sense that some good or evil is about to happen. For when we sense an omen in this unthinking way <note>original: "inconsulto," meaning spontaneously or without prior deliberation.</note>, we seem to be moved by a natural motion; and the motion of nature, as Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371 – c. 287 BC), a Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle in the Peripatetic school.</note> and Themistius <note>Themistius (c. 317 – c. 388 AD), a statesman and philosopher who wrote extensive commentaries on Aristotle.</note> have taught, is derived from an intelligence that does not err. 

Indeed, intelligence moves us through the influence of the heavens' motion. Therefore, that unexpected omen-taking will serve as proof to us that the omen-bearing quality is natural, and does not happen entirely by chance or accident. An example of this for us is Deiotarus, King of Cilicia <note>Deiotarus (c. 105 – 40 BC) was a ruler of Galatia and Cilicia known for his strict adherence to the signs of birds; Cicero famously defended him in a speech.</note>, who, when he had seen an eagle descending onto a roof, immediately abstained from using that building, taking an omen from the sight. On the following night, the roof collapsed. 

It is clear, therefore, that nothing can be omen-bearing for us unless we have taken the omen from that certain natural impulse. Thus far regarding the chosen hour, through which...

->### BOOK I.###<-

<sig>b 2</sig>

it is clear that an omen <term>augurium: a sign or prophecy, originally derived from the observation of birds</term> should be accepted as a single sign, even if it occurs several times, as long as it happens while the moon remains in the same zodiac sign; for all these occurrences possess the same power.

### *Regarding the time when the event will occur.*

Consequently, let us speak of the time in which the event will take place. Astronomers <note>original: "Astronomi." In this period, the terms for "astronomer" and "astrologer" were often used interchangeably to describe those who studied the influence of the heavens on earthly events.</note> teach that the events which an omen portends are of two kinds: either those that can happen in a short time, such as within an hour or a day—like the arrival of a guest, the meeting of a friend, and things of that sort—or those which cannot happen within a day or an hour, but may occur within a month or a year, such as war, mass death <note>original: "mortalitas," often referring specifically to a plague or epidemic.</note>, and other matters of that nature. 

Therefore, when the event is one that can happen in a short time, it should be predicted that it will occur before the moon leaves the sign through which it was traveling at the time the omen emerged. If, however, it cannot occur in a short time, they believe it will happen before the moon has traversed that same sign twelve times <note>Meaning twelve lunar months, which constitutes a full solar year.</note> from when it held it at the time of the omen—that is, within a year. For example, if the significance of the omen concerned a Pontiff <term>Pontificis: a high-ranking religious leader, often the Pope</term>, or a Prelate <term>Præsulis: a high-ranking church official or dignitary</term>, or...

Regarding the death of a Patriarch or a King, they believe this comes to pass before the moon travels twelve times through the zodiac sign <note>Meaning a full lunar year of twelve months.</note> which it occupied at the moment of the omen. So much for the timing of events. Having clarified these points, it remains to discuss auguries <term>auguries: signs or omens, often interpreted from the behavior of birds or other natural phenomena</term> and sneezing, which the vanity of the pagans <note>original: "gentilium uanitas." The author likely uses this term to distinguish these ancient superstitions from his own religious worldview.</note> believed to be certain indicators of the future.

### Regarding prophetic sneezes

Aristotle, a man of such great learning, while speaking of sneezing in his books on natural history, asserts that an omen from a sneeze is a holy and sacred sign. He points to the moment where Penelope <note>The wife of the hero Ulysses (Odysseus), who remained faithful during his long absence after the Trojan War.</note> laments the loss and injury which the suitors <note>The "proci," a group of noblemen who moved into Penelope's home to pressure her into marriage while Ulysses was missing.</note> were inflicting upon her household. She added that the cause of all these troubles was the absence of her husband, Ulysses; if he were to return, he and his son Telemachus would easily repay that injury. As soon as she had said this, Telemachus immediately sneezed with such a loud noise that the whole house echoed. Penelope interpreted this to signify the coming slaughter of the suitors. Homer records that a sneeze is holy and sacred in the 17th book of the *Odyssey*, as Plutarch also mentions in his *Life of Homer*. Theocritus writes the same in his *Wedding Song for Helen*. From this, the vanity of the pagans—

BOOK I.
<sig>b 3</sig>

[...vanity], they consider quite a few superstitions regarding sneezes. For many nations <note>original: "gentes plurimae"</note> report that if someone is thinking of doing something and happens to sneeze two or four times, it is an augury <term>augury: a sign from the gods used to predict the future</term> that the matter they are undertaking will be brought to a successful conclusion. If, however, someone should sneeze more than four times, the ancients <note>original: "prisci"</note> recorded it as being neither a good nor a bad sign. 

But if someone should sneeze either once or three times, they should cease both speaking and acting entirely; for they report that the matter will not reach a good end either quickly or easily. If two people sneeze at the same time, they should proceed with what they had planned to do, for they report this is a prosperous augury, both in matters conducted by sea and by land. 

If two people sneeze twice at the same time, they should follow through with what they have begun, for they report it is the best possible augury for both deeds and words. If someone arrives in a new place for the first time and sneezes once, they will bring the matter they had proposed to do to a successful conclusion. 

They claim that a sneeze made at night by anyone who is in the house portends profit; however, two sneezes portend a loss. Regarding the significance indeed of him...

-># ON THE SIGNS OF AUGURY<-

<meta>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of sneezing omens.</meta>

...they claim that whoever sneezes at night participates <note>original: "participare"</note> [in such profit]. They report <note>original: "tradunt"</note> that two sneezes occurring during any night, made by anyone who is in the house, portend that some person from the house will die, or that there will be a loss in the house, or even a very great profit. 

The Egyptians also said that when someone begins to inhabit a house for the first time, and while entering the house they sneeze once, they may inhabit it safely; but if they sneeze twice, they should by no means enter it. 

While a person is lying in their bed, if they are awake and sneeze once, an evil omen is portended, as it announces either sickness, a loss, or something of that nature. If, however, they were sleeping and sneeze immediately upon waking, it is far worse. For it foretells <term>presages: to give a sign or warning of a future event</term> either great sadness, the death of a person, a very serious hindrance, or the loss of one's belongings. 

But if someone, while lying in bed and waking up, sneezes twice, they will achieve something good, such as profit or health—if they were ill—and if they were sleeping [at the time], they will attain something even better. 

Truly, if someone sneezes twice every night until the third [night], then toward the best...

...possible outcomes will proceed, because it leads either to profit or to honors. **If someone** should travel for a whole day or part of a day for the sake of business or trade, and has rested in some lodging <term>inn or guesthouse: original "hospitio"</term>, and sneezes there twice, let him change his location; for in the other lodging to which he moves, he will prosper. If he remains in the first, he will suffer a loss. **When** someone seeks out a certain place for the purpose of conducting some business, and then sneezes once, let him depart from that place immediately, for he will only prosper in another location. But if he sneezes twice, he may safely move his belongings from that first place to another. **If** someone, having established an agreement for some matter, sneezes once, they consider it certain that the pact is firm. But if he sneezes three times, the pact will be weak <note>original: "infirmum"</note>. **If** someone on Monday morning <note>original: "die lunae"</note>, when rising from bed, sneezes once, he will prosper in profit or in some other matter for that entire week. On the contrary, if he sneezes twice [it portends the opposite]. **If** someone has lost a horse, or a ring, or any other thing, and while leaving the house to search for that thing, sneezes once, he will recover the lost item.

DE AVGURIIS
->Concerning Auguries<-

But if he sneezes twice, he will never find the item. On **Sunday** <note>original: "Die uero dominica"</note>, however, if someone rising from bed sneezes three times, it is a good sign; but if only once, he will encounter bad luck. Before lunch or dinner, when someone has begun to eat, if he sneezes twice, he will find it to be a good thing, but if once, it is a bad omen.

Aristotle <note>abbreviated as "Ari."</note> records, and physicians have proven, that for sick men who despair of their lives, a single sneeze <margin>(curved mark)</margin> occurring is fatal, but two are a sign of health <note>original: "salutare"</note>; for women, however, it is the opposite, for one is a sign of health, but two are fatal. To one traveling through a forest, two sneezes occurring announce an escape from bandits <note>original: "latronibus"</note>, but one, truly, is an evil sign. These are the things which the Egyptians wrote concerning auguries <term>omens or divinations: original "auguriis"</term> taken from sneezes; there are also others which I omit, as they seem far more vain than those we have already mentioned.

### Concerning the augural twitches and tremors of each small part of the body.

BOOK I.
<sig>b 5</sig>

...ing of the limbs <note>original: "tus membrorum", completing "saltus" from the previous page</note> are augural <term>prophetic: relating to the interpretation of omens</term>, which even Theocritus <note>A famous Greek pastoral poet who also wrote about folk beliefs</note> proved, taking our beginning from the very crown <term>apex: the highest point or crown</term> of the head. For if the crown of the head trembles, it foretells sorrows and troubles, or a long journey to come; yet for a slave, it foretells injuries; for a maiden, a husband; for a widow, sorrows. 

According to other authors, it decrees an encounter with evils, but for a maiden, marriage—since, as they say, Jupiter <note>original: "Iuppiter"</note> gave birth to Minerva <note>original: "Minervā"</note> from his own crown <note>Referring to the myth where Athena/Minerva was born fully armed from the head of Zeus/Jupiter</note>. For a widow, it likewise means marriage; for a wealthy man, loss and plots; for a soldier, happiness; for a sailor, a storm—though those who suffer a storm in the crown of their head will be survivors. 

Truly, if the head is shaken, or if the hairs stand on end, or if they shudder <note>original: "horreant"</note> at any time, they indicate persistent plots for a long time, or portend harm from some friend or blood relative; for a slave, however, they omen sickness; for a maiden, blame; for a widow, injury; but for the poor and those in need, they omen good things. For the rich, they omen weakness and an unusual <note>original: "insolentiam"</note> way of life. For the sick, truly, they omen life. Others transmit the tradition differently, for they say if the head trembles, it means bad...

### ON AUGURY <note>original: "DE AVGVRII S", likely "De Auguriis"</note>

...harm <note>original: "lum", completing the word "malum" from the previous page's catchword</note> lasting for a long time; it announces death for a slave, and loss for a master and a widow. For other people, it indicates subjugation, and then again freedom. If the whole head should throb <term>throb: 'salierit', literally to leap or jump</term>, it presages death, yet for others it announces many good things. 

But if the back part of the head as far as the hollow <note>referring to the nape of the neck or the occipital hollow</note> [throbs], it generally signifies an enemy, plots, and a particular death; but for the rest, it signifies adversity; for a slave, good; for a maiden, a good husband; for a widow, sickness; for a wealthy person, danger; and for a farmer, labor. 

If indeed the right side of the head throbs, it omens good: joy and freedom for a slave; blame <note>original: "vituperationem"</note> for a maiden; and for a widow, good things and the continuation of her widowhood. 

But if it is the left side, it signifies loss; for a slave, a change of household; for a maiden, blame; for a widow, insult. 

After these, concerning the brain. For a twitching brain <note>original: "cerebrum saliens"; likely referring to a pulsing sensation felt at the crown of the skull or the fontanelle</note> denotes illness to one who is healthy, but health to the sick, as well as growth of the body and sustenance; but for the sterile, it denotes a son and joy; for the exile, wandering and a return to his fatherland; but for the soldier and the sailor, it denotes danger to the head <note>original: "capitis periculum"; this could imply a physical head injury or a "capital" legal threat to one's life</note>. 

Next, concerning the forehead. For if the forehead should tremble, in matters...

### ON AUGURIES

...to suffer danger, blame for a maiden, and slander for a widow. If the extreme end of the right part [of the nose throbs], it signifies usefulness, but for a slave, a maiden, and a widow, it means unpleasantness. If the middle between both extreme parts of the nostril <term>nostril: "naris," which can refer to the nose or the nasal opening</term> should tremble, it portends loss and injury, but for a slave, good consideration <note>original: "considerationem," likely meaning the slave will be looked upon favorably by their master</note>, and for a widow, help.

**Regarding the cheeks, Melampus says.** If the right [cheek] <note>original: "mala," referring specifically to the upper cheek or cheekbone</note> is shaken, it presages an unexpected injury, for a slave something good, and for a maiden injury; but the left [cheek] always signifies something good, for a slave a long journey abroad, for a maiden censure, and for a widow utility. Afterward:

**Regarding the lips.** If the upper lip should throb <term>throb: "salierit," the leaping or twitching of a muscle</term>, it signifies a crisis <note>original: "discrimen," implying a decisive turning point or a moment of danger</note> and victory; for a slave something good, and for a maiden profit. **But if** it is the lower lip, it signifies utility; however, according to the Egyptians <note>the text often cites Egyptian traditions as a separate authority from the Greek Melampus</note>, it signifies loss of work.

**And regarding the jaws after this.** For if the right [jaw] should throb, it signifies utility, that a slave will be entrusted with the management of the household <note>original: "fidem oeconomiae," the trust required for a steward or household manager</note>, sickness for a maiden, and censure for a widow. But if it is the left [jaw], it announces sadness in a small matter; for a slave utility, for a maiden censure, and for a widow good consideration.

# BOOK I.

**Regarding the cheeks.** <note>original: "genis," referring to the sides of the face</note> If the right [cheek] is shaken, it signifies good speech, for a slave a safe passage <note>original: "vadum," literally a ford or shallow place, suggesting a safe crossing or a secure journey</note>, for a maiden counsel, and for a widow utility. But if it is the left, it signifies suffering evil, for a slave disease, for a maiden injury, and for a widow unpleasantness. **Let us speak of the ears.** If the right ear should throb <term>throb: "salierit," referring to a sudden involuntary twitch or pulse</term>, or if it should ring, it signifies joy in some matter, for a slave good conversation, for a maiden acquisition <note>original: "acquisitionem," often meaning a gain in property or a proposal</note>, and for a widow something good. But if the left [ear throbs], it signifies something lofty being done, for a slave the undertaking of great things, for a maiden reproach, and for a widow a loss <note>original: "diminutionem," implying a reduction in status or resources</note>. But if the inner part of the right ear should throb, they affirm it to be a bad sign; of the left, however, one will hear something, yet will not be glad of it, though it announces something good for a slave.

**And regarding the chin.** For if the right part of the chin should throb, it signifies the acquisition of goods for everyone. The left announces both good hope and good things for all. But if the whole [chin throbs], it presages extreme old age.

**Regarding the palate.** If the palate should throb, it signifies good speech within the home, and if it is the left part, it is also good; on the contrary, however, if it is the right part <note>implying the right palate is a negative omen, contrasting with the left</note>.

# BOOK I.

->**OF THE SIGNS OF AUGURY**<- <note>original: "DE AVGVRII S," likely an abbreviation for "De Auguriis Signis" (On the Signs of Augury)</note>

**Regarding the teeth**, they report: if the teeth tremble, it announces discord, but also something good.

**Concerning the mouth**, they say that if the mouth shakes, it is a sign that one will see someone after a long time whom they will then associate with. **Regarding the heart**, they say that a palpitating heart portends good for everyone; according to others, it signifies cheerfulness or a friend's snares <note>original: "insidias," referring to a trap or ambush</note>. For a slave, however, it signifies freedom; for a maiden, laziness; for a widow and a soldier, good speech; and for a merchant, business.

**Regarding the neck:** if the right part of the neck should throb <term>throb: "salierit," literally "to leap," describing an involuntary muscle twitch</term>, it signifies fear for a free man and sickness for a slave. For all others, it is good if the left side trembles.

**Concerning the throat**, if the right part of the throat shakes, they affirm it signifies good speech, and for a slave or a freedman, profit. But if it is the left part, it signifies the flight of unpleasantness for all; for others, free glory; a slave will hear bad things; for a soldier, the acquisition of money; for a maiden, unpleasantness; and for the pilot of a ship, praise.

**Regarding the collarbone** <note>original: "iugulo," referring to the hollow of the throat or the collarbone area</note>, if the right part of the collarbone trembles, it signifies something good; for a slave, however, it signifies anxieties <note>original: "cogitationes," literally "thoughts," but in this context often implying worries or deep concerns</note>; for a maiden, boldness; for a widow, labor; for a soldier—

...others say [it is the finger of Saturn], for others it means harm, for a freedman an incision <note>original: "incisionem"; likely referring to a surgical cut or a wound</note>, for a maiden profit, for a widow utility. If the last finger, which is called the *anticheir* <note>Greek: ἀντίχειρ, "against the hand," referring to the thumb</note>, should twitch <term>twitch: "salierit," a sudden involuntary movement interpreted as an omen</term>, it signifies the acquisition of many goods, and joy in children and wives; for it is, as they say, the finger of Venus <note>original: "Veneris digitus"; in ancient and Renaissance palmistry, the thumb was associated with Venus, the goddess of love, representing passion and willpower</note>, for a slave something good, for a maiden a husband, and for a widow utility.

### Concerning the nails.
If the nails of the right hand should twitch, it foretells unexpected profit, for a slave good speech <note>possibly meaning a successful plea or being spoken well of by a master</note>, and for a maiden boldness. If the nail of the small finger of the right hand should twitch, it announces the suffering of evil, for a slave a son, for a maiden a husband, and for a widow labor. But if the nail of the second finger <note>counting from the pinky: the ring finger</note> should twitch, it portends power, for a slave the death of his master, for a maiden ignominy, and for a widow something good. If, indeed, [the nail] of the third <note>middle</note> and fourth <note>index</note> finger [should twitch], it speaks of sadness, but for a slave something good. If, indeed, [the nail] of the large finger <note>the thumb</note> [should twitch], it announces an increase of life. If the small finger of the left hand should twitch, it foretells many good things for everyone. If the second finger <note>the ring finger</note> [should twitch], [it foretells] sadness and a response from great persons, and for a slave who is faithful, an accusation. If the middle finger [should twitch], [it foretells] sadness through one's own affairs...

**Concerning the breasts.** If the right breast should tremble, it presages a great work, and for others something new and good. But if the left [trembles], riches. If the middle between the breasts [trembles], it is a sign of good thoughts. **Concerning the chest.** If the chest should twitch <term>twitch: "salierit," a sudden involuntary muscle movement interpreted as an omen</term>, it announces a banquet for everyone, but for others the fulfillment of a journey <note>original: "discursus"; this can refer to a physical trip, a conversation, or the completion of a sequence of actions</note>, though with fear. **Concerning the stomach.** If the stomach should jump, it portends that a rich man will have to make expenditures, for a poor man food, and for others something new and good. **Concerning the belly.** If the belly <note>original: "aluus"; referring generally to the abdomen or bowels</note> should twitch, it announces good things, and for others useful sons. If the right part of the belly [twitches], it tells of a disease in a short time. **Concerning the twitching of the flanks.** If the right flank <note>original: "ilium"; the area of the lower abdomen and hip</note> should twitch, it portends a journey abroad. For others, if the left [flank twitches], something new and good; for another, it presages business. **Concerning the sides.** If the right side should palpitate, it presages long-lasting poverty for a rich man, but for a slave, means and joy; for others, utility. If the left [side twitches], it portends a good status for a laborer <note>original: "laboranti"; referring to one who works or is in a state of distress</note> for his entire life.

->### ON OMENS ###<-

**Concerning the spleen.** If the spleen should <term>twitch: "salierit," a sudden involuntary muscle movement interpreted as an omen</term>, it announces sickness. **Concerning the liver.** If the <term>liver: "epat"</term> should tremble, it stirs up anger.

**Concerning the hip.** If the right part of the hip <note>original: "ischii"</note> is shaken, it signifies a quarrel with members of the household or friends, but for others, something good. But if the left part [shakes], it tells of sadness, and for others, instability.

**Concerning the spine.** If the right part of the spine should throb, it signifies an insult. If indeed the left [throbs], it announces a banquet for a rich man, but labor for a poor man.

**Concerning the penis.** If the <term>penis: "mentula"</term> should twitch, it portends for a slave that he will go to Venus <note>original: "ad Venerem ire"; a common classical euphemism for sexual intercourse or seeking a lover</note>.

**Concerning the testicles.** If the right testicle is shaken, it signifies the acquisition of sons and of a livelihood. But if the left [shakes], it portends a banquet and usefulness.

**Concerning the buttocks.** If the right [buttock] should twitch, it announces business to one possessing integrity <note>original: "incorruptionem habenti"; this could refer to a person of moral character or perhaps one in a state of physical health</note>. If the left, for a rich man it portends making expenditures, and for others something good. But if the right joint of the hip together with the buttocks should twitch, it presages a move with happiness, and for others something good. If the left, it foretells the removal of sadness, but for others a lack of delight.

# ON AUGURY

...resources; for a virgin, a future marriage; for a widow, benefit. If the nails of the left foot should twitch or tingle <note>original: *formiculaverint*; literally "to crawl like ants," referring to a pins-and-needles sensation</note>, it announces unpleasantness to the rich, riches to the poor, loss to a slave, counsel to a virgin, and disgrace to a widow. If the whole body shakes, it portends being watched for several days; for a slave it signifies that no traps are being set; for a virgin it signifies that no danger will occur. These are the superstitions regarding the tremors that occur in our bodies, which Melampus the <term>Sacred Scribe</term> <note>original: *Hierogrammateus*; a title for an Egyptian priest-scholar responsible for holy texts</note> observed, and after him Antiphon and Phimonoes the augurs, who received them from the Egyptians, as Melampus writes. They have handed down no cause for these vanities—neither a true one nor even a plausible <note>original: *coloratam*; literally "colored" or "varnished," implying a reason that is made to look attractive or convincing</note> one—but merely approved superstitions of this kind through their observations. We shall most clearly rise up against these things in the following book. So much for augural tremors.

### BOOK I.

After these things, we must discuss those matters which, by their own nature, bring good or bad omens to men. The authors of these traditions were Caras <note>likely a reference to the legendary Carian inventors of augury</note>, Orpheus, Amphiaraus, Tiresias of Thebes, and Amphiction, who was also the first to interpret both portents and dreams. I do not intend to recount everything that is said, as they are innumerable, but only certain ones as examples, since our intention is not to prove these things, but rather to refute them.

However, it must first be understood that the Romans attributed such great faith to auspices that they would not even appoint magistrates unless the auspices advised it. For that reason, as Messala <note>Marcus Valerius Messalla Rufus, a Roman consul and author on augury</note> records, some magistrates were considered "lesser" and others "greater," because the lesser were created through lesser auspices and the greater through greater auspices, a point which Gellius <note>Aulus Gellius, a 2nd-century Roman author</note> explains in Book 13, Chapter 14.

Festus Pompeius writes that the augurs observed five kinds of signs: from the sky, from birds, from two-legged animals, from four-legged animals, and from <term>dire portents</term> <note>original: *diris*; signs that were inherently ill-omened or terrifying</note>.

<margin>Funeral birds.</margin> Among the birds, they called those "funeral birds" which, in the context of auguries, forbade something from being done. The "oscinum" augury <term>oscinum augurium</term> <note>from the Latin *oscines*, birds that provide omens through their song or voice rather than their flight</note> was taken from the singing of birds, [and the birds were called] osci...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of bird-related omens (augury) from the previous page. -># ON AUGURY<-

<margin>Singing birds</margin> ...singing birds <note>original: *oscines*; those that provide omens via their voice</note> are all those birds that make an auspice with their mouth. They used to call a bird "supervacanea" <note>original: *supervacaneam*; meaning superfluous or redundant</note> when it emitted its voice from the highest peak. Concerning birds, therefore, Aristotle handed down in his books *On Animals* that vultures are augural signs; for, as Pliny says in Book 10, Chapter 6, they fly about two or three days before to the place where corpses are going to be. It has been proven by experience that this was the case during the destruction of the Trojans, as Aristotle mentions in his writings on augury. The Romans used the vulture most of all, because twelve appeared to Romulus at the founding of the city; or perhaps because, as Herodotus says, the vulture harms no living animal, as it eats nothing but <margin>Augural Birds.</margin> corpses. Hylas the augur records that the augural birds are the Little Owl, the Eagle Owl, and the *Asio* <note>a species of eared owl</note>, which is called *Otos* <note>Greek for "eared"</note> because it has ears standing out with feathers. Among these, the Roman writers Massurius and Mutius listed the Woodpecker (which hollows out trees), the *Sanqualis*, and the *Immussulus* <note>these are ancient names for specific birds of prey; the *Sanqualis* was associated with the god Sancus and is often identified as an osprey or a young eagle</note>. Pliny explains what the *Sanqualis* and the *Immussulus* are in Book 10, Chapter 7. The augural birds include the entire genus of hawks, and most especially the Eagle. Also the Raven, the Crow, the Wood Pigeon, the Swan, the Rooster, the Hen, and those insects which fly...

### BOOK I. 47

...[fly] <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of birds that fly in groups</note>, as do Bees, Ants, and Locusts, about which *Apion* <note>Apion was a 1st-century Greek-Egyptian grammarian known for his works on Homer and Egyptian history</note> writes extensively. <margin>Vultures</margin> The Romans used the vulture most of all in their auguries <note>the practice of predicting the future by observing birds</note> because it is seen so rarely; for no one easily finds a vulture’s nest, as they arrive suddenly and unexpectedly. Thus, their appearance always signifies something significant. *Aristotle* and *Pliny* always interpreted them as an evil omen. *Romulus*, however, made use of twelve vultures as a good sign when founding the city <note>Referring to the legendary contest between Romulus and Remus to determine who would name and rule Rome</note>. For a similar reason, as *Herodotus* writes, *Hercules* was delighted by the sight of a vulture when beginning any endeavor, judging it the most "just" of all flesh-eating birds <note>original: *iustissimam*; this refers to the belief that vultures do not kill living prey, but only eat those already dead</note>. Yet, the common opinion of men is that vultures flying in flocks near armies are a prophecy of coming slaughter. For vultures and eagles can sense corpses from across the seas; flying very high, they see from above things that are hidden by the shadows of many mountains—and not only things present, but even, as *Pliny* says, what will happen in two or three days. ### CONCERNING AUGURY
<meta>catchword: especially</meta>

<margin>Crows</margin>
...[especially] <note>original: "praesertim"; this completes the thought from the previous page regarding the behavior of crows</note> the augurs <term>ancient diviners who interpreted the will of the gods through the flight and behavior of birds</term> attributed this to future destruction. Among the Indians, there is a certain region now called Pharcelos where a great multitude of ravens perished; when a far larger multitude returned there later, it signified the desolation of the region and the destruction of the people who lived there. The story of Valerius Corvinus's raven <note>Marcus Valerius Corvus was a 4th-century BC Roman general who was allegedly aided by a raven that perched on his helmet and attacked his Gaulish opponent during a duel</note> is well known; indeed, it was a good and fortunate omen for Valerius, so much so that he was named "Corvinus" <note>derived from "Corvus," the Latin word for raven or crow</note> because of it. For this reason, all who write about the auguries of birds that feed on carcasses—such as Vultures, Eagles, Crows, Jackdaws, and Kites—briefly report that when a flock appears in a certain region and stays there for several days, it is a sign of future ruin. In Upper Swabia, near the castle of Risum <note>likely referring to the fortress of Reisensburg in modern-day Bavaria</note>, a great flock of ravens settled, where they were seen to gnaw at the walls of the town and the houses; they did not leave that place until the lord of the area died, along with many others who lived in the town. **It is reported**

# BOOK I.

...[especially] <note>original: "praesertim"; completing the sentence from the previous page</note> playing and remaining in a state of joy. <margin><unclear>intro</unclear></margin> For he asserts that when it [the eagle] flies in such a manner, it offers so much good in auguries <term>divination through the observation of birds</term> that nothing further could be added. The fight of those eagles that appeared during the speech of Telemachus <note>the son of Odysseus/Ulysses</note> to the suitors <note>the men pressuring Penelope to marry while her husband was missing</note> was interpreted by Halitherses—a hero in Homer’s works and the most skilled of the augurs of his age—as meaning that Ulysses would return to his homeland and that it signified the slaughter of the suitors. 

An eagle—a tame one—seized a goose from a nearby courtyard with its talons and released it before the chariot in which Telemachus was being carried; Helen <note>Helen of Troy, who appears in the *Odyssey* after her return to Sparta</note> interpreted this to mean that Ulysses, having been driven about by long wanderings and exhausted by many calamities, would finally return to his homeland and visit the extreme punishment of death upon all the suitors. 

Penelope interpreted another sign: an eagle that unexpectedly rushed upon twenty geese she was raising at home, killed them all, and flew away; she interpreted this to portend the return of Ulysses and the death of the suitors. 

<margin><unclear>alinpa</unclear></margin> When an eagle carrying a dove in its talons flew in from the left, Amphinomus <note>considered the most well-behaved of the suitors</note> interpreted it to mean that the suitors' expectations for their plots would not be met. 

<meta>catchword: interpreted</meta>

# ON THE SUBJECT OF AUGURY

...was interpreted. <note>original: "tatus est"; completing "interpretatus est" from the previous page</note> When the Greeks had received as a favorable omen an eagle dragging a fawn <note>original: "hūmulum," likely referring to "hinnuleum" or a small animal</note> in its talons and dropping it near the altar dedicated to Jupiter Panomphaeus <note>Jupiter as the source of all oracular voices and omens</note>, they were restored in spirit—which by then had entirely collapsed—to such a degree that they brought about a great slaughter of the Trojans. 

<margin>Homer</margin>
Polydamas <note>a seer and friend of Hector in the *Iliad*</note> interpreted another sign: an eagle flying from the left, carrying in its talons a snake that was resisting it fiercely. Because the eagle was unable to carry the snake back to its chicks, Polydamas said it portended that the Trojans would not entirely destroy the [Greek] ships—even if Hector utterly rejected faith in such auguries <term>divination by the flight and behavior of birds</term>.

<margin>Hawks</margin>
Indeed, [the scholar] Apion regarded the predatory act of any hawk as a bad omen; for he says that such rapacity portends that a person setting out on a journey will be killed by robbers. However, if the prey should escape from the hawk’s "hands" <note>talons</note>, Apion believes the traveler will likewise escape. 

Of the various hawks, Pliny writes in Book 10, Chapter 8, that the *Circon* <note>the *kirkos* or harrier hawk</note>, which is lame in one foot, is an omen of the greatest prosperity for marriage business and financial matters. 

<margin>Homer's *Odyssey*</margin>
The hawk, the messenger of Apollo, was seen dragging a dove in its feet, which it was plucking during the journey Telemachus had made from his ship; 

->Theoclymenus<- <note>a prophet who interprets this sign in the *Odyssey* as a symbol of the enduring power of Odysseus's family line</note>

# BOOK I.

...plucking its feathers <note>original: "plu..." completing "plumante," the act of plucking feathers</note>. Theoclymenus <note>a seer and descendant of Melampus in the *Odyssey*</note> interpreted this as a sign that the lineage of Telemachus <note>son of Odysseus</note> would continue to lead in the administration of the kingdom, and predicted that the rule would remain with the Ithacans. Furthermore, regarding the buzzard—which is called the *Triorchis* <term>literally "three-testicled," an ancient folk etymology for a type of hawk</term> because of the number of its testicles—Phemonoe <note>the mythical first priestess of Delphi</note> granted it the primary place among the bird-omens.

<margin>Crow</margin>
The crow, as Pliny says in Book 10, Chapter 12, is a talkative bird that does not provide a formal augury <term>a sign from the gods used to approve or disapprove of a specific action</term>, although it is praised by some in matters of divination. 

Ravens, however, are the only birds in the auspices <term>signs taken from the behavior of birds</term> that seem to possess an understanding of the significance of their own signs. For, as Pliny says in the same passage, their most terrible omen is when they "swallow" their voice as if they were being strangled. 

<margin>Little Owl. Eagle Owl.</margin>
The little owl, the eagle owl, and the screech owl provide the worst possible omens, especially regarding public affairs. For they inhabit deserted places—not only desolate spots, but those that are dire and inaccessible—and they do not sing with any melodic voice, but rather with a groan. When an eagle owl entered the inner chamber of the Capitol <note>the Temple of Jupiter Optimus Maximus in Rome</note> during the consulship of Sextus Papinius Allenius and Quintus Plautius <note>the OCR reads "Papellio Istro" and "Pediano," referring to the consuls of 36 AD</note>, the city of Rome had to be purified <note>original: "lustrata est"; a ritual cleansing to avert divine anger</note> in the month of March, as Pliny records. 

<margin>Hens.</margin>
As for hens and wood-pigeons, auguries are not usually taken from them, except those derived from their eggs. Indeed, Livia Augusta <note>the wife of Emperor Augustus</note>, in her early youth when she was pregnant with Tiberius Caesar by her first husband Nero, used a "girlish" omen because she so greatly desired to give birth to a male child...

# OF AUGURIES

They say a hen with a small laurel branch fell into the lap of Livia Drusilla, who was the Empress, and it has been preserved to this day that the Emperors were crowned with laurel from that very plant <note>This refers to a famous omen where an eagle dropped a white hen into Livia's lap; the hen held a laurel sprig which was planted and grew into a grove used for imperial crowns.</note>.

<margin>Livia</margin>
<margin>Swan</margin>
Regarding the swan, sailors have received an augury, for they say this bird portends tranquility, as Aemilius <note>likely Aemilius Macer, a Roman poet who wrote on birds</note> writes when he says, "The swan is always the most joyful bird in the auspices <term>signs from birds used to predict the future</term>." Sailors hope for this bird because it does not submerge itself in the waves.

<margin>Cock</margin>
Concerning the Cock <term>the male of the domestic fowl, or rooster</term>, Pliny writes in Book 10, Chapter 21, that it provides auguries; for he writes that they possess portents within themselves, and he mentions their preposterous <note>here meaning "contrary to nature" or "out of the usual order"</note> evening songs. Assigning a cause for this, he says that by crowing through entire nights, they foretold that noble victory of the Boeotians <note>residents of a region in central Greece, specifically referring to the Battle of Leuctra</note> against the Spartans <note>original: "Lacedaemonios"</note>; the interpretation was conjectured thus because a defeated bird would not crow. It is even numbered by the Romans among divine auguries, for it is found in the annals in the Ariminensian <note>referring to Ariminum, the modern Italian city of Rimini</note>
