# BOOK I.

...in the territory of Rimini. During the consulship of Marcus Lepidus and Quintus Catulus <note>78 BCE</note>, a cock was said to have spoken in the villa of Galerius.

<margin>Woodpecker</margin>
The Woodpecker <note>original: "Picus"</note> was also accepted in the study of auguries by the Pagans <note>original: "Gentilibus," referring here to the ancient Romans and Greeks</note>, and for this reason it took its name from the son of Saturn <note>In Roman mythology, Picus was a king of Latium and son of Saturn who was turned into a woodpecker by the sorceress Circe</note>, who used the bird in his auspices. For they say this bird possesses something of the divine, because in whatever tree it has built its nest, a nail or whatever else has been fixed there cannot remain for long, but immediately falls out as soon as the bird sits there.

<margin><unclear>Albertus</unclear></margin>
<margin><unclear>Albertus</unclear></margin>
Albertus <note>referring to Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century scholar known for his writings on natural history</note> reports that if the hole in which the woodpecker has its nest or its young is blocked with a bolt, the woodpecker, bringing a certain herb, unblocks the hole by the power and touch of that plant. 

On the head of the city praetor <term>a Roman judicial official</term>, Lucius Tubero, while he was administering justice before the tribunal in the Forum, a woodpecker sat so calmly that it could be captured by hand. The seers <term>prophets or soothsayers</term> responded that the destruction of the empire was portended if the bird were released, but if it were killed, it meant destruction for the Praetor. He immediately tore it to pieces, and not much later he fulfilled the omen <note>Tubero died shortly after, but the Roman Republic was saved from the immediate threat</note>. 

Homer, in the tenth book of the *Iliad*, records that this same bird is an augural bird; for when Ulysses and the son of Tydeus <note>Diomedes</note> were about to scout what was happening in the camp of the Trojans, Pallas <note>Athena</note> sent it forth...

<margin>Toad</margin>
...toad <note>completing the word "bufo" from the previous page</note>, a land frog of immense size; Virgil [writes], "And the toad found in the hollows" <note>original: "Inuentusque cauis bufo" from Virgil’s *Georgics* 1.184; the OCR "canis" is a likely transcription error for "cauis" (hollows)</note>. For since auguries are the divine wills of the gods, they are often delivered to men through those things which are unpleasant at first glance. 

<margin>Boar</margin> 
Melampus <note>a legendary Greek seer and healer</note> also recorded the **boar** <note>original: "aprum"</note> as a bad omen; for when it is encountered by someone setting out, it foretells the destruction of those things they intend to do. 

<margin>Serpent</margin> 
The Romans accepted the **serpent** <note>original: "serpentem"</note> as an omen. For Lucius Sylla <note>Lucius Cornelius Sulla, a famous Roman general and statesman</note>, having set out for Samnium <note>original: "Samium", likely referring to the region of Samnium in Italy during the Social War</note>, led his army against Nola. The people of Nola, however, had established their camp outside the walls with troops and defenses. Therefore, while Sylla was consulting the gods about the battle, a snake suddenly appeared. Having questioned the <term>haruspex</term> <note>a religious official who interpreted omens from nature or entrails</note> as to what that snake portended, the response was that he should invade the enemy camp with great courage; having done this, he achieved victory. Furthermore, in Homer's *Iliad*, Book II, while the Greeks were sacrificing, a serpent crawled forth from the altar into a nearby plane tree...

# ON AUGURIES
### 56

...in which he <note>referring to the serpent mentioned on the previous page</note> took eight sparrow chicks along with the mother, who was the ninth; **Calchas** <note>the primary soothsayer of the Greeks during the Trojan War</note> interpreted the augury, saying that the war which the Greeks had then undertaken against the Trojans must be extended into the ninth year.

<margin>The Hare.</margin>
The **hare** <note>original: "lepus"</note> was also accepted into the study of auguries; for instance, it appeared in the army of **Xerxes** <note>the Great King of the Persian Empire</note>, King of the Persians, which he had prepared against the Greeks in numbers so vast as to be nearly incredible. A mare gave birth to a hare, which was intended to signify the most shameful flight of the king that followed afterward; for the hare is a most timid animal, always presaging flight when it appears to armies.

<margin>Appearing fire and flame.</margin>
The pagans <note>original: "gentiles"</note> also accepted appearing **fire and flame** as a most fortunate augury, and not only birds and wild beasts. We read of this regarding the flame of **Servius Tullius** <note>the legendary sixth king of Rome, said to have been born a slave; a flame allegedly danced around his head while he slept as a child without harming him</note> which appeared around his head while he slept, and concerning the flame of **Lucius Marcius** <note>Lucius Marcius Septimus, a Roman officer whose head reportedly spontaneously erupted in flames while he encouraged his troops during the Second Punic War</note>, as recorded in the first book of **Valerius** <note>Valerius Maximus, a Roman author famous for his collection of historical anecdotes</note>.

<margin>The division of auguries.</margin>
Therefore, omitting other things that could be said, this division of the subject is gathered: that some auguries are those which are bad or good by their very nature; others are those which can be either good or bad depending on the locations, positions, times, and intentions with which they appear and are received.

# BOOK I.
### 57

Furthermore, there is another division of signs: some are **falling omens** <note>original: "caduca auspicia"</note>, occurring when something falls down within a temple; others are **diverting omens** <note>original: "deuia"</note>, which prohibited an action from being performed; others are **land-based omens** <note>original: "pedestria"</note>, which are given by a fox, a wolf, a serpent, a horse, or any other four-legged animal; others are **atoning omens** <note>original: "piacularia"</note>, which portended grim outcomes for those performing sacrifices—such as when a victim fled from the altar, or let out a bellow when struck, or fell upon a part of its body other than what was proper; and finally, others are **fatal omens** <note>original: "pestifera"</note>, such as when a heart was not found within the entrails <note>original: "extis"; the internal organs of a sacrificial animal used for divination</note>, or a "head" <note>a specific lobe or protrusion</note> was missing from the liver.

**Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of the "Natural History"</note>, a most learned man in the field of augury, grants them a great deal of credit. However, he asserts in Book 28, Chapter 2, that the power within portents and auguries is within our own control. For he believed they hold power only insofar as they are accepted by us; if we accept a sign as a good thing, he affirms it is a good omen, but if we take it as a bad thing, it is a bad omen. If we do not pay attention to these things, or do not accept them as an augury, they will be omens of neither good nor evil; for he did not...

<meta>This page completes the discussion on Pliny the Elder’s views regarding the observer's intent in augury and introduces Plutarch’s philosophical explanation for why the left side is considered lucky in Roman divination.</meta>

ON AUGURIES
<meta>catchword: right</meta>

...certain birds, nor does he say that any auspices <note>divine signs or omens</note> pertain to those who, when entering upon any matter, have denied that they observed them. For this reason, he writes that in the discipline of the augurs <note>the formal rules and training of the Roman priests who interpreted omens</note>, no auspices apply to those who have neglected to observe them while setting out.

### The Opinion of Plutarch of Chaeronea
### *regarding auguries.*

**Plutarch of Chaeronea**, however, in his book of *Problems* <note>original: "libro problematū"; referring to Plutarch's work *Roman Questions*, which investigates the origins of Roman customs</note>, affirms that auguries are good or bad in respect to us and our physical position. For when a sign related to augury <note>original: "res augurifica"</note> occurs to us, which we accept as an omen, we must take note of its position. For all things on the right are bad, while those on the left are truly good. He offers the reason for this: they considered earthly and mortal things to be contrary and opposite to divine and heavenly things. Therefore, he believed that those things which are on our left, the gods send forth from their right; whereas those things on our right, the gods send from their left. And since those things which the gods extend to us from their right are meant for our benefit—for "to give the right hand" is to offer help—it happens that all things which occur to us on the left are given for our good by the gods. Conversely, those which [come] from...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Plutarch’s interpretation of augural signs, specifically defining the physical movements that classify a sign as being on the "right" or "left," and begins a description of how Roman augurs observed the sky from the Old Curia.</meta>

BOOK I.

[on the] right. However, an augural sign <term>augurifica; a thing relating to the art of the augur</term> is said to be "on the right" in two ways: through passage and through rest. Regarding passage: first, if from the front an augural sign moves from the left side to the right; second, if similarly from behind it moves from the left to the right. In both of these cases, it is called a right-handed omen. Third, if it comes from behind and passes by our right side into the area in front of us: in this way also the augury is called right-handed. Conversely, by these same methods but in the opposite direction, an augury is called "left-handed" by its passage and motion. 

Regarding rest: if we encounter an augural sign <note>original: "augurificae rei"</note> while it is stationary, resting either on the right or on the left; or if it is not resting but comes toward us and then settles either on the right or the left. In all these ways, the augural sign is called right or left. And whenever it is on the left, **Plutarch** judges that it should be interpreted as a good sign, whereas when it is on the right, it is always interpreted as a bad sign.

*Otherwise*, the *Roman augurs* also assigned different positions in the heavens; for when they placed themselves in the highest part of the *Old Curia* <note>original: "curiæ ueteris"; the ancient meeting house of the Roman Senate or a specific sacred site used for taking auspices</note>...

...they had received [the signs], dressed in priestly garments, holding the **lituus** <note>original: "lituum"; a curved wand without knots, the primary tool of the Roman augur used to mark out the ritual space of the sky</note>—which was a staff—raised in their hand, they would mark out a space in the sky by its circuit, which they would watch, into four divisions: the left, from the east; the right, from the west; the front, toward the south; and the back, toward the north; and they said that the distinctions between auguries were received with respect to these four quarters.

### *The Opinion of Astronomers on the Efficacy of Auguries.*

**Astronomers** <note>In this context, the author likely refers to astrologers or those studying the influence of celestial bodies</note>, however, provide good or bad interpretations for augural signs based on the positions of the heavens. Then they say that in the marking of this circle, there are four points: the **East**, which inclines toward the rising of the world; the **West**; the **South**; and the **North**, which are to be marked according to the positions of the world. They assert, therefore, that the East belongs to the **Sun**, the West to **Saturn**, the South to **Mars**, and the North to **Mercury**. Furthermore, the partitions...

### The opinion of the younger augurs regarding augury.

<margin>Prosperity</margin>
The younger augurs, however, have reduced all matters of **augury** <term>auguralia: the practice of interpreting signs and omens from nature</term> into twelve **superstitions** <note>original: "superstitiones"; here used as a technical term for categories of signs or ritual observations rather than a pejorative for irrational belief</note>, which are perceived in relation to ourselves. Of these, the first is called **Prosperity**. **Prosperity** occurs when someone leaves their home for the sake of transacting any business; for when a person, a wild beast, a bird, or something of that kind first meets the one leaving the house on their journey—whether the creature walks or flies—and if it positions itself first on the left side, they assert that it is a good sign for the business to be transacted and completed.

<margin>Misfortune</margin>
The second superstition is called **Misfortune**. This is when someone leaves their home for the sake of transacting some business, and while going they encounter a wild beast, a person, a bird, or something of that kind...

For if someone, while walking or standing to conduct some business, sees an **augural** <term>augurificam: relating to the practice of augury, or divining the future through signs</term> sign—such as a man, a wild beast, or a bird—cross from the left to the right, and it vanishes once it reaches that side, they believe it portends good for the matter at hand.

<margin>Fortune</margin>
iiii. The fourth superstition is called **Fortune**. This occurs when someone finds a thing moving or flying that settles before them on the right side in the sight of the one seeking an **augury** <term>augurium: a sign from the gods interpreted through the behavior of animals or nature</term>; for they claim such an augury is good for the completion of the task.

v. The fifth superstition is called **Misfortune**. This occurs when someone finds an augural sign resting on their right side and looking toward the person seeking the augury; for they say this is an evil **omen** <note>original: "omen"</note> regarding the business to be conducted.

<margin>Loss</margin>
The seventh **superstition** <note>original: "superstitio"; used here as a technical classification for a ritual observation rather than "false belief"</note> is called **Loss** <note>original: "Damnum"</note>. It occurs when you see an **augural** <term>augurificam: relating to the practice of augury, or divining the future through signs</term> sign resting or standing behind you, and specifically on your right side; it is called "loss" because it provides a bad sign for completing the task. 

viii. 

<margin><unclear>Caution</unclear></margin>
<margin>Caution</margin>
The eighth superstition is called **Caution** <note>original: "Cautela"</note>. It occurs when you see an augural sign behind you, and before it reaches you, or you reach it, it rests somewhere or stands watching you. For whether it rests on the right or the left, as long as it has not passed beyond you, it foretells good for the matter at hand. 

ix. 

<margin>Misfortune</margin>
The ninth superstition is called **Misfortune** <note>original: "Infelicitas"</note>. This is when you see an augural sign passing you by, yet it then rests or stands somewhere, and specifically on the left. This is called "misfortune" by the younger school because it offers an evil **omen** <note>original: "omen"</note> for the matter to be done. 

x. 

<margin>Perfection</margin>
The tenth superstition is **Perfection** <note>original: "Perfectio"</note>. This occurs when an augural sign comes from your left and passes to the right, which you see neither resting nor standing, but rather vanishing; for it is called "perfection" because it is a good and perfect sign for the matter at hand.

<margin>Imperfection</margin>
xi. It is called **Imperfection** <note>original: "Imperfectio"</note>, and it occurs when an **augural** <term>augurifica: relating to the practice of augury, the interpretation of divine will through signs like bird flight</term> sign passes from your right side to your left behind your back, and you see it either standing still or resting. This superstition is called "imperfection" because it is a bad **omen** <note>original: "omen"; a sign portending good or evil</note>.

<margin>Conclusion</margin>
xii. Finally, the opposite occurs from the left to the right, which is called **Conclusion** <note>original: "Conclusio"</note>, because they affirm that this is the best possible sign for the matter being undertaken. These are the twelve superstitions which the **augurs** <term>augures: official Roman diviners</term> claim to be true in matters of augury, provided that the intention of the one taking the **auspices** <term>auspicantis: the person observing the signs to determine if an action has divine approval</term> and the time of the observation are maintained, as we have discussed above.

### Superstitions of the Romans regarding Auguries.

Among the Roman augurs, as **Plutarch** <note>Plutarch (c. 46–119 AD), a Greek-Roman historian and philosopher; the reference is to his work "Roman Questions"</note> affirms in his *Problems*, there were other superstitions, the first of which was the **invocation of the gods** <note>original: "Deorum invocatio"</note>. For the priests did not chant the augural formulas unless the gods had first been invoked and humbly entreated. ii. The second superstition concerned the identity of the people performing the invocation; for auguries—as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of the "Natural History"</note> says in Book 8, Chapter 29—were a matter for the **college of priests** <note>original: "collegium... sacerdotum"; the official body of Roman priests responsible for religious law</note> and were conducted with the utmost solemnity...

# CONCERNING AUGURY

<meta>catchword: Augustus</meta>

...of the augurs. iii. There was a rule regarding the quality of the person: for individuals suffering from sores or ulcers were not accepted among the **priest-augurs** <term>augures: official Roman diviners who interpreted the will of the gods by observing the flight and behavior of birds</term>, nor were those who possessed any "bite" <note>likely referring to a sting of conscience or a sharp grief</note> or disturbance in their soul. For since it was believed that they handled divine matters, they thought the mind of the gods would only benefit humanity when the priests were of a clear and sound spirit. 

iv. There was a superstition regarding the augural birds: for the same reason, they did not accept diseased or ulcerated birds into the **auguries** <term>auguria: the practice of divination</term>, as it is not fitting to observe crippled or mutilated creatures for the performance of divine rites. 

v. There was a superstition regarding the ritual requirements: the priests did not seek auguries unless open lamps were present. This is because a lamp stands in relation to a priest as the soul does to the gods—for light represents the spirit that dwells within. There is also another reason provided by **Plutarch** <note>Plutarch (c. 46–119 AD) discusses this in his *Roman Questions* (76), asking why the augurs used lamps</note>: since augural birds do not stay still when winds are blowing, the presence of wind is detected by lighting lamps and torches <note>original: "luminaribus accensis"</note>. **Metellus** <note>Likely Lucius Caecilius Metellus, who was High Priest (Pontifex Maximus) in 243 BC and famously saved the Palladium from the burning Temple of Vesta</note>, the **High Priest** <term>Pontifex Maximus: the chief high priest of the College of Pontiffs, the most important religious position in ancient Rome</term>, a wise and civic-minded man, after the month of **Sextilis** <note>The sixth month of the Roman calendar, later renamed August in honor of Augustus Caesar</note>

<summary>This page details the ritual purity required for Roman augury, noting that both the priests and the birds had to be physically and mentally unblemished. It also explains the symbolic and practical use of lamps during the observation of omens.</summary>

BOOK I.

He forbade taking **auspices** <term>auspicari: the act of observing birds to discern the divine will</term> during the month of August <note>original: "Augustum"; likely referring to the month rather than the Emperor, given the seasonal context that follows</note> because it is a matter of wisdom to take auspices with adult and healthy birds before the summer has passed. In the autumn, however, the birds are partly useless, partly diseased, and partly still immature chicks; some also fly away to other places because of the time of year.

->*On the number of augural objects according to those augurs who have spoken more reasonably about auguries.*<-

As we have said, there are four things necessary in auguries according to the practice of the **Gentiles** <note>The author uses 'Gentiles' here to refer to the ancient Romans or pagans</note>: first, the objects that occur unexpectedly to the one taking the auspices, beyond their original intention, which are called **augural things** <note>original: "augurales res"</note>. Second, the person taking the auspices, to whom such objects present themselves unexpectedly and beyond their intention. Third, the intention of the one taking the auspices, by which these augural things are interpreted as an augury. And fourth, the time in which they occur. And because the Gentiles have spoken superstitiously about augural objects, it remains now to speak of such objects according to those who have spoken less superstitiously about them, so that nothing is overlooked.

# ON AUGURY <note>original: "DE AVGVRII S"; likely an abbreviation for "De Auguriis" (On Auguries)</note>

...in the opinions of the ancients. These are contained in twelve chapters, corresponding to the number of signs described in the **Zodiac** <term>Zodiacus: the belt of the heavens divided into twelve signs, used here to categorize different types of omens</term>.

The first chapter concerns the entry of animals into a house, whether they be domestic <note>original: "urbanæ"; literally 'of the city' or 'of the home'</note> or wild animals. For when an animal enters someone's home both contrary to habit and without intention, philosophers have classified it as an **augural object** <note>original: "augurificũ obiectum"; a physical event or thing interpreted as a divine sign</note>.

**ii.** The second chapter concerns beasts that encounter us unexpectedly on a journey.

**iii.** The third is the burning of garments, when it happens unexpectedly from a fire as if flying toward them; to this chapter, lightning strikes and the setting on fire of a house or any other thing are also referred.

**iiii.** The fourth is a mouse gnawing something that is dear to us, such as a book, clothing, or gold—especially that in which we place our hope. To this chapter are also referred: a **wolf** devouring our ox or horse; a **dog** devouring something we cared for; and a **fox** stealing a **hen** or a **goose**, and other things of that kind.

**v.** The fifth is domestic sound; for it is believed that due to noises and crashes in the house, which

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion initiated at the end of the previous page, moving from a list of omens into an analysis of the "unerring intelligence" and various classical and medieval theories regarding the source of prophetic signs.</meta>

# BOOK I.

Theophrastus, whom the brilliant teacher Themistius follows, stated that the cause moving these signs is an **unerring intelligence** <note>original: "non errantem intelligentiam"</note>. For those things which happen to us by chance and at random, they believed occurred by **providence** <note>original: "prouidentia"</note> if they are attributed to divine intelligence—both Themistius and Theophrastus held this view. From this, Themistius judges that humans speak of future things even if they understand nothing of what they say, to the extent that they are moved by that divine intelligence itself.

This divine intelligence, which is called "unerring," is also the **Soul of the World** <note>original: "mundi anima"</note>, which Plato in his dialogue *Timaeus* demonstrated to have arisen from the **secondary gods** <term>In Platonic philosophy, these are lesser deities created by the Demiurge to fashion the mortal parts of the universe and humanity</term>. 

Avicenna, however, attributes this to the **intelligence of the Moon** <note>In medieval philosophy influenced by Arabic scholars like Avicenna (Ibn Sina), the "Active Intellect" was often associated with the celestial sphere of the Moon, governing the terrestrial world</note>; for this intelligence moves and creates all things contained below the Moon, especially everything ordered toward the benefit of humanity. 

Others, however, have said that God is the cause which moves these things; Valerius <note>Likely Valerius Maximus, a Roman author known for his collection of historical anecdotes</note> seems to have held this opinion when he says it must be believed that these matters consist not of accidental motion but of divine providence. The Pythagoreans also believed these signs came from God himself, so much so that they believed auguries to be...

<meta>catchword: God:</meta>

It cites a lineage of thinkers from Plato and Theophrastus to the medieval philosopher Avicenna.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of divine communication, moving from the general concept of "unerring intelligence" discussed on page 71 to specific classical theories about why gods or spirits provide signs of the future.</meta>

# ON AUGURIES

They called them the **voices and words of the gods**; for since God has a care for us, it is consistent with reason—as **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle in the Peripatetic school</note> says—that He predicts to us something of the future, so that a good thing may be hastened or an evil thing avoided. By this reasoning, Theophrastus, along with the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of the mystical and mathematical philosophy of Pythagoras who believed in the harmony of the universe</note>, affirms that all omens and auguries are the voices and words of God, through which God foretells coming good or evil to humanity. From this, **Themistius**, as **Averroes** <note>Also known as Ibn Rushd, a famous 12th-century Islamic philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were essential to Western scholarship</note> relates in the 12th book of the *Metaphysics*, records that there were many people who, moved by God Himself, predict future events, even though they do not understand what they are saying.

### *On the moving cause of auguries according to the Platonists.*

**Hermes Trismegistus** <note>original: "Mercurius Trimegistus"; a legendary figure often credited with ancient Egyptian-Greek wisdom and the Hermetic writings</note>, however, asserted that the power of augury is moved by a **daemon** <term>In classical philosophy, a 'daemon' is an intermediary spirit between gods and mortals, acting as a guide or messenger, and is not necessarily "demonic" in the modern sense</term>. **Porphyry** <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonic philosopher and student of Plotinus</note> explained that this movement comes from a **calodaemon** <note>original: "calodemone", derived from the Greek *kalos* (good) and *daimon* (spirit)</note>, which is called a **good genius** <note>original: "bonus genius"; a protective spirit assigned to a person at birth</note>. He explained that two spirits are assigned to every human being for their guardianship: a **cacodaemon** <note>original: "Cacodemon", from the Greek *kakos* (evil) and *daimon* (spirit)</note> indeed, who urges one toward evil, and a **calodaemon**, who urges one toward good; of these, one is called the good spirit and the other the evil spirit. When indeed

<summary>This page details classical theories on the origin of omens, specifically how God or intermediary spirits (daemons) communicate future events to humans. It introduces the Platonic concept of the "calodaemon" and "cacodaemon" as the driving forces behind these signs.</summary>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "calodaemon" (good spirit) as the moving cause of auguries. It transitions from the Neoplatonic views of Porphyry and Plotinus to the astrological theories attributed to the Roman scholar Varro.</meta>

->BOOK I.<-

[Since] an omen is something good, by which we either avoid a future evil or obtain a future good, it follows that it is stirred up by a **Good Spirit** <term>original: "Calodaemone"; in Neoplatonic thought, a 'calodaemon' is a benevolent spirit that acts as a guide or messenger from the divine</term>. This opinion was favored by **Plotinus** <note>A 3rd-century philosopher and the founder of Neoplatonism</note> and **Iamblichus** <note>A student of Porphyry and an influential Neoplatonic philosopher who wrote extensively on the soul and divine mysteries</note>, as is evident in the books they wrote concerning spirits.

### *On the moving cause of auguries according to the followers of Varro.*

Certain very learned men record that they have accepted from the decrees of **Varro the Roman** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), an immense figure in Roman scholarship whom Cicero called "the most learned of the Romans"</note> that auguries are moved by the **Moon** as it wanders through the twelve signs of the **Zodiac**. They say that the Moon is the cause, and indeed the only cause, which moves all things—including auguries—partly because she alone is the nearest to us among the stars, and she alone (as the **Astronomers** say) possesses power over those things which happen suddenly. This is most especially due to her speed; for this reason, she governs things that are moist and fluid. Since auguries and all such signs occur suddenly, it happens that in such matters, she alone provides the driving force.

->ON AUGURIES<- <note>original: "DE AVGVRII S."</note>

### *The Opinion of the Egyptian Astronomers*
### *regarding the cause that triggers auguries.*

The Egyptians, however, claim that it is not the Moon, but rather a wandering star from among the five planets <note>original: "stellā errantem de quinq; planetis"; in ancient astronomy, the five planets known besides the Sun and Moon were Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> which moves our minds toward taking auspices <term>original: "auspicandum"; the ancient practice of interpreting signs, particularly from the behavior of birds, to determine the will of the gods</term>. They argue that the "augury-making" <note>original: "augurificus"</note> planet is the one which, at the hour of the omen, occupies the ninth, tenth, first (the horoscope), seventh, fourth, or third house in the figure of the heavens. Specifically, the planet found in the ninth house of the sky at the hour we receive augural signs is the one that creates the omen. However, if no planet is present in the ninth house, the "augury-making" planet will be the one in the tenth house; if not in the tenth, the one in the horoscope <note>the first house, or the sign rising on the eastern horizon</note>; if not in the horoscope, the one in the seventh; if not in the seventh, the one in the fourth; and if not in the fourth, finally, the planet in the third house is the one that moves both us and the augural events themselves toward the auspices.

The quality of the augury, moreover, arises from the lordship of the ascendant, as well as from that specific planet. For if both are joined to one another, whether by "aspect" <note>the geometric angles between planets, such as a trine or square</note> or by "body" <note>a physical conjunction where planets appear to meet in the same degree of the zodiac</note>, they believe that nothing evil will result from the auguries. Where, however, the lord of the ascendant <note>The text breaks off here as the author prepares to describe unfavorable astrological conditions.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the Egyptian astrological theory of augury, which identifies specific planets in various "houses" of the sky as the triggers for omens and human intuition. It contrasts this with the Roman view of the Moon's influence, focusing instead on a hierarchy of celestial positions starting with the ninth house.</summary>

BOOK I.

...the [lord of the ascendant] and the augury-making planet are not joined by any relationship, but one of them has a favorable configuration with the **benefics** <note>original: "beneficis"; refers to planets like Jupiter and Venus, traditionally considered helpful in astrology</note>, then good is portended from the auguries. But if it is with the **malefics** <note>original: "maleficis"; planets like Saturn and Mars, traditionally considered harmful</note>, evil is pronounced. Moreover, the good or evil that is portended will be according to the nature of that house in which they are joined in body, or look upon one another by **aspect** <note>the geometric relationship or angle between two planets</note>. For if it were the house of wealth <note>the second house of the zodiac</note>, good or evil is portended in one's finances. But if it were the seventh house, good or evil regarding a wife; and if the fifth house, regarding children; and in the rest of the houses, they pronounce good or evil by a similar reasoning.

### *On the moving cause of augury according to a less / superstitious explanation.*

It must therefore be said, as Porphyry <note>a Neo-Platonic philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) who wrote on astrology and philosophy</note> states, that if any cause can be devised for those things which happen by chance, the "moving cause"—which is the principal one, as was the opinion of certain **Pythagoreans** <note>followers of Pythagoras who sought the spiritual and mathematical order of the universe</note>—is the **Calodaemon** <term>original: "Calodæmonem"; from the Greek "Kalos Daimon," meaning a "Good Spirit" or "Good Genius"</term>. He is also called the "good genius," for he persuades man toward good things, a view which the **Platonists** <note>philosophers following the traditions of Plato</note> certainly affirm by consensus. However, the **instrumental** <note>the secondary cause or "tool" used by the moving cause to produce the sign</note> cause...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spiritual causes of omens. This page explores the Moon's role as the physical instrument used by these spirits to communicate with humanity.</meta>

-># ON AUGURIES<-

The moving cause of augury is the motion of the Moon through the twelve signs of the heavens—an opinion held by the **Followers of Varro** <note>original: "Varronici"; refers to the school of Marcus Terentius Varro, an influential Roman scholar of the 1st century BC</note>. This view, though perhaps superstitious, seems less jarring than others. For, as **Astronomers** admit, the Moon holds power over all things that happen suddenly and unexpectedly—precisely the nature of auguries and omens. The **Good Spirit** <term>original: "Calodaemon"; a protective "Good Genius" or spirit assigned to a person, often associated in astrology with the 11th House</term>, as we stated in the book on demons, is established for the benefit of humanity. It acts through a natural instrument because it is more powerful than man, yet weaker than the forces of the heavens. Since a more powerful agent uses fewer instruments—as **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher (384–322 BC) whose logic and natural philosophy underpinned medieval and Renaissance science</note> says—it follows that the spirit moves all omens and auguries through the course of the Moon alone, using it as its primary tool.

That a spirit uses the stars in this way is evidenced by **Haly Abenragel** <note>original: "Hebenragel"; a famous 11th-century Arab astrologer whose "Book of the Skilled in the Judgments of the Stars" was a standard textbook in the West</note>. He recounts that a son was born to a certain king on a day when four planets were joined together in the specific sign that was rising in his **birth chart** <note>original: "genitura"; the configuration of the stars at the moment of birth</note>. On that very day, the infant spoke and predicted the loss and ruin of his father the king. It is not, however, consistent with nature for a child so recently born

<keywords>Moon, Calodaemon, Varro, Haly Abenragel, Aristotle, omens, astrology, birth charts, divination</keywords>

## BOOK I.

...[he] could not have spoken except by the power of a **spirit** <term>original: "daemon"; in this context, it refers to a guiding supernatural entity or "genius," rather than an evil being</term>, who used that heavenly constellation to make predictions. For perhaps that spirit was **Mercurial** <note>original: "Mercurialis"; belonging to the nature of the planet Mercury, the Roman god of communication, speech, and intellect</note>. Indeed, in the eighth degree of **Libra**, which was the **rising sign** <term>original: "horoscopabat"; the point of the zodiac ascending over the eastern horizon at the moment of birth, often called the Ascendant</term> at that time, Mercury presided. Venus, Jupiter, Mercury, and Mars were all joined together in that eighth degree; however, none of them except Mercury held the **astrological terms** <note>original: "fines"; specific sub-divisions within a zodiac sign where a particular planet is thought to have increased influence</note> in that degree. Therefore, that spirit was of a Mercurial nature.

If this is true, the spirit who guards us is able to set in motion all omens and augural signs for our benefit—stirring them into action—by using only the motion of the Moon through the twelve signs of the zodiac. The aspects or configurations of the other stars, however, neither help nor hinder this process, as they possess no power in such matters of augury. Thus far we have discussed the "moving cause." Now, so that this current opinion may be fully understood, let us turn to the examples of the tables,

Many other examples could occur, but these twelve shall suffice. For the augur <note>An "augur" is a diviner or soothsayer who interprets signs and omens to predict the future.</note> who is strong in intellect can be led from these examples to many other conclusions.

Therefore, the interpretation will be as follows: if a beast should enter the house while the Moon is holding Aries, it signifies that there will be strife and slander. ON AUGURY

### Table of twitches according to the Egyptian
### astronomers.

There are those who have reduced the twitches <note>original: "saltus"; literally "leaps" or "jumps," referring to involuntary muscle spasms or tics which were interpreted as prophetic omens.</note> of every small part of the body to twelve, according to the number of parts and members of our body which are subject to the [Zodiacal] signs. This was the method of the Egyptian astronomers, among whom are Petosiris and Nechepso <note>Petosiris and Nechepso were legendary figures in antiquity, often cited as the foundational authors of Hellenistic astrology and divination.</note>. For they attributed the head to Aries, the neck and the nape to Taurus, the shoulders and arms to Gemini, the chest to Cancer, the sides and stomach to Leo, the belly to Virgo, the kidneys and vertebrae to Libra, the private parts to Scorpio, the thighs to Sagittarius, the knees to Capricorn, the shins to Aquarius, and the feet to Pisces.

If, therefore, the head or some part of the head twitches, they said that all this pertains to the head and must be attributed to Aries; and they taught the same regarding the rest of the body. Whence, if the head or some small part of the head should twitch while the Moon is holding <note>original: "tenente"; meaning the Moon's current position in a specific sign of the Zodiac.</note> Aries, it signifies that there will be a lawsuit and slander. If the Moon is in Taurus, it signifies that a guest has arrived. If in Gemini, that diseases are occurring in the region; and the same must be said for the rest in their proper order. It is, however, worthy of note that while the Moon holds the same sign, it is possible that that member...

<meta>catchword: member</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the astrological system of 'palmomancy,' where muscle twitches are interpreted as omens based on which Zodiac sign governs that specific body part and where the Moon is currently positioned.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, melothesia, Petosiris, Nechepso, muscle twitches, palmomancy, Zodiac signs, lunar influence</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how muscle twitches (palmomancy) are interpreted based on the Moon's position. It specifically addresses the repetition of these omens and introduces sneezing as a similar divinatory practice.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>
<sig>f 5</sig>

...member, or a small part of that same member, should jump <note>original: "saliat"; referring to the leaping or twitching of a muscle.</note> not just once, but twice or several times, and therefore all these twitches will have the same meaning <note>original: "vim"; literally "power" or "force," referring to the prophetic significance.</note> until the Moon moves itself into another sign. For as long as the same cause remains, the same effect <note>original: "affactus"; a term meaning the impression or influence made upon the body.</note> must necessarily remain. This is to be observed not only in twitches, but also in the other types of augury <note>technical term: "auguriis"; signs or omens used to predict the future.</note> that are recorded in the previous tables.

In a similar way they spoke of sneezes; for they reduced all sneezes occurring by night and day to twelve categories according to the number of [Zodiacal] signs. However, they derived the power of significance from the very first of all the sneezes, whether one or many occurred on the same day and night; because until the Moon has left the sign under which the first sneeze happened, they all have the same meaning, as they are to be considered as a single event. We shall note this in the following tables which serve for
->twitches and sneezes.<-

<summary>This page explains the temporal rules of palmomancy and sneezing as omens, stating that multiple occurrences carry the same prophetic meaning as long as the Moon remains within a single Zodiac sign. It establishes that the first occurrence defines the omen's message for that entire period.</summary>
<keywords>palmomancy, sneezing, astrology, Moon signs, Zodiac, omens, divination rules</keywords>

### *The author's observation, by which superstitious auguries are rejected.*

Since those things whose credibility was gained through observation cannot be more effectively rejected by any other means, such vanities must be refuted by the very observations through which they first took hold in the minds of men. For instance, [it is noted that] the <term>head of the liver</term><note>original: "caput iecoris." For <term>King Pyrrhus</term><note>Pyrrhus of Epirus (319–272 BCE), a Greek king and general who famously fought Rome; his "victories" were so costly they gave rise to the term "Pyrrhic victory."</note>, on the day he died, the heads of the sacrificial victims were found to have been cut off; for this reason, people placed faith in <term>entrails</term><note>original: "extis." Also for the Divine <term>Augustus</term><note>The first Roman Emperor (r. 27 BCE – 14 CE).</note>, while sacrificing at <term>Spoleto</term><note>original: "Spoleti." The prophetic response was that he would double his empire within a year. Likewise, twin livers appeared to the Divine Augustus on the day he was victorious at <term>Actium</term><note>The decisive naval battle in 31 BCE where Augustus defeated Mark Antony and Cleopatra.</note>.

From these and similar observations, therefore, a fuel for the madness of such great men emerged—yet this belief is refuted by the many more and even greater observations that have been made. <term>Quintus Mucius Scaevola</term><note>A famous Roman jurist and Chief Pontiff (Pontifex Maximus) murdered in 82 BCE during the civil wars of Sulla and Marius.</note>, a most eminent <term>augur</term><note>A religious official who observed and interpreted omens.</note> among the leading men of all ages, was killed by <term>Sulla’s</term><note>Lucius Cornelius Sulla, a Roman dictator.</note> executioners while he was embracing the very altar in the Temple of <term>Vesta</term><note>The goddess of the hearth; her temple was one of the most sacred sites in Rome.</note>—than which nothing in the city of Rome was considered more holy. This occurred contrary to the opinion he himself held regarding his own auguries. <term>Nero</term><note>The fifth Roman emperor, notorious for his superstitions and eventual suicide.</note> also, though he clung excessively to all the vanities through which the future is predicted, was never safe from an end that he did not expect through the auguries in which he trusted. Of those Romans who clung to these vanities, whose actions and outcomes did not turn out as interpreted through auguries by the seers—I shall pass over them with a steady gaze. Instead, let us approach those things which have been observed by us personally: for in my youth, which is the most inquisitive age, I most diligently scrutinized all the record-books which I had annotated. I examined the book on **sneezes** <note>original: "sternutamentis." In antiquity, the timing or direction of a sneeze was often interpreted as a divine omen of approval or warning.</note> and the book on **twitches** <note>original: "saltibus." This refers to *palpitatio*, the involuntary leaping or twitching of muscles, which was studied in a form of divination called palmoscopy.</note>, and all the rest; and I found them for the most part to be false, and only very rarely true.

Reason teaches us that the meanings of those auguries written in such books occur by chance, falling under the category of rare coincidence. Sneezes, twitches, and other natural occurrences—which were believed to bring us auguries by their own inherent natures—are superstitious and vain. Indeed, although we observed them very frequently, we never found any truth in them.

The observations of <term>Plutarch of Chaeronea</term><note>A famous Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46–119 CE) who wrote extensively on Roman customs and religious rites.</note>, who defined auguries through **positions**, are not only vain but even legendary; for having tested them more than once, we found none of them to be true.

### *The Peripatetic Argument by which*
### *auguries are rejected.*

Let us therefore first rise up against such augural superstitions with the arguments of the <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>The followers of Aristotle, known for their focus on logic, observation, and natural causes.</note>; for if auguries announce future events, it is either because they are the causes of those events, or because they are their effects. But it is established that auguries are neither the causes nor the effects of future things; therefore, auguries do not predict the future except <term>at random</term> <note>original: "temere." This implies a lack of rational or necessary connection; any "hit" is merely a coincidence.</note>. For we see that a physician predicts future health from effects <note>Symptoms in the body are the "effects" of an underlying disease, allowing a doctor to predict the outcome.</note>, and the astronomer indeed by the stars, which are the causes of celestial effects. But since auguries are not the causes of future outcomes, nor are they their effects, it rightly follows that future things are predicted through auguries only by chance.

Added to this is the fact that a dream is no more the cause of a future event than an augury is, for both are related to a future effect <term>by accident</term> <note>original: "per accidens." In Aristotelian logic, this refers to a coincidental relationship rather than a direct, causal one (per se).</note>. According to <term>Aristotle</term> in his work *On Sleep* <note>original: "Arist. de somno." Referring to the treatise *On Sleep and Sleeplessness* or *On Prophesying by Dreams*, where Aristotle argues that most "prophetic" dreams are mere coincidences.</note>, a dream is the cause of a future thing only by accident; for it may happen while I am dreaming that <term>Plato</term> comes to me, and so Plato truly arrives, just as it happens that as I am leaving my house, a flash of lightning occurs; for the lightning is not...

...of going out from the house is either the cause or the effect <note>The text here completes a sentence from the previous page, likely discussing how leaving one's home (an "obfuscation" or movement) has no causal link to a future omen.</note>. To this we add that the imagination, by which I imagine a friend is coming, is no more the cause or the effect of that friend's arrival than the cry of a <term>crow</term> <note>original: "cornicis." ->*The evasion of certain insane people.*<-

Truly, certain insane people try to escape these arguments by saying that auguries, *as* auguries, predict the future by chance, but that auguries performed under such-and-such a movement of the Moon predict future events <term>by their nature</term> <note>original: "per se." The author is mocking those who try to save the reputation of augury by linking it to the "scientific" study of astrology.</note>. They argue that although auguries, as mere signs, are neither the causes nor the effects of future things, nevertheless, auguries performed under this or that course of the Moon are "co-effects" of future events. ### *The Argument of the Peripatetics by which auguries are rejected.*

Let us therefore first rise against such augural superstitions with the arguments of the **Peripatetics** <note>The Peripatetics were followers of Aristotle’s school of philosophy, known for their focus on natural philosophy and rigorous logic.</note>. For if auguries announce future events, it is either because they are the **causes** of those events, or because they are their **effects**. But it is well-established that auguries are neither the causes nor the effects of future things; therefore, auguries do not predict the future except by chance. For we see that the **physician** predicts future health from effects <note>i.e., symptoms that have already manifested in the body.</note>, while the **Astronomer** <note>original: "Astronomum." In this period, "astronomy" and "astrology" were often used interchangeably to describe the study of the stars' influence on Earth.</note> predicts through the stars, which are the causes of superior effects. But since auguries are not the causes of future effects, nor are they the effects of those causes, it rightly follows that future things are predicted through auguries only by chance. Added to this is the fact that a **dream** <note>original: "insomnium"</note> is no more the cause of a future matter than an augury is; for both are compared to a future effect **by accident** <term>by accident</term> <note>original: "per accidens." This is a technical term in logic for a connection that is coincidental rather than essential or causal.</note>. But according to **Aristotle** in *On Sleep* <note>Referring to Aristotle's treatise "On Prophesying by Dreams" from the *Parva Naturalia*.</note>, a dream is an accidental cause of a future event. For it happens that while I am dreaming that **Plato** is coming to me, it [actually] happens that Plato arrives; just as it happens that as I am leaving my house, a **flash of lightning** <note>original: "coruscatio"</note> occurs; for neither is the flash of lightning

<meta>catchword: [light]ning</meta>

<summary>This page uses Aristotelian logic to argue that augury has no scientific basis because there is no causal link between a sign (like a bird's cry) and a future event. The author compares the "coincidental" predictions of augurs to the way people occasionally experience dreams that seem to come true by sheer chance.</summary>

It concluded by comparing auguries to dreams, which Aristotle argued are often merely coincidental.</meta>

BOOK II.

[...of the stumbling] <note>original: "-tuscationis," likely the completion of "offensationis" (stumbling) from the previous page. Stumbling while leaving one's house was a common ancient omen.</note> that occurs when leaving the house is neither the cause nor the effect of the outcome. To this we add that the imagination, by which I imagine a friend is coming, is no more the cause or the effect of that friend’s arrival than the song of a crow is the effect or cause of an enemy’s arrival. For in both cases, the connection is accidental and fortuitous; therefore, if a friend happens to arrive while I am imagining it, he arrives by chance, just as an enemy happens to appear by chance while a crow is singing. Thus, auguries appear to predict the future only **by accident** <term>by accident</term> <note>original: "per accidens." In Aristotelian logic, this refers to a coincidental connection rather than a natural or necessary cause-and-effect relationship.</note>, just as **Aristotle** <note>Referring to Aristotle’s *On Prophesying by Dreams*, where he argues that most "prophetic" dreams are merely coincidental.</note> says dreams themselves do.

->*The excuse of certain foolish men.*<-

Certain foolish people <note>original: "insani," literally "insane," but here used as a polemical term for those with illogical beliefs.</note> indeed attempt to escape these arguments by saying that while auguries, as mere auguries, predict the future by chance, auguries performed under this or that movement of the **Moon** do predict the future of their own accord. They argue that although auguries, as auguries, are neither the causes nor the effects of future events, nevertheless an augury performed under a specific lunar course is a **co-effect** of those future events. Their reasoning is that the augury itself (performed under a certain Moon) and the future events it portends both originate from the same cause—namely, from the—

# ON AUGURIES

...by the course of the **Moon** <note>original: "Lunae decursu." The author is finishing a thought from the previous page.</note>, just as although fiery urine <note>A medical reference to dark or "burning" urine, often used in medieval diagnosis.</note> is neither the cause nor the effect of a **choleric fever** <term>choleric fever</term> <note>In ancient medicine, a fever caused by an excess of yellow bile, or "choler."</note>, nevertheless because that fiery urine and the choleric fever originate from the same cause—namely, from a **choleric humor** <note>One of the four bodily fluids (humors) believed to govern health and temperament.</note>—therefore that fiery urine portends the choleric fever, and conversely the choleric fever portends the fiery urine.

->Refutation of this evasion.<-

But this evasion seems frivolous, for in this way we could approve every superstitious divination. For I might say that **Geomancy** <term>Geomancy</term> <note>A method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil/rocks.</note>, **Pyromancy** <term>Pyromancy</term> <note>Divination by means of fire.</note>, and other such practices are true, because although points arranged in such a way are neither the effects nor the causes of future events in themselves, yet when cast under this or that phase of the Moon, they will be co-effects of future events. By the same reasoning, even **lots** <term>lots</term> <note>original: "sortes." The practice of drawing lots (sortition) to determine the divine will or future outcomes.</note> would have efficacy, because I could always say that even if they are not the direct causes or effects of future events, they are nevertheless co-effects occurring from the same Moon. But it is well-established that lots are rejected by everyone, since they predict future events blindly and not through any power of their own.

<summary>The author refutes the argument that divination is valid if signs and events are "co-effects" of the Moon, arguing that this logic would absurdly validate all forms of superstition like geomancy and pyromancy.</summary>

BOOK II.

<meta>catchword: Phauo</meta>

### The argument of Saint Thomas by which auguries and lots are refuted.

The most holy Doctor **Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (c. 1225–1274), a preeminent medieval philosopher and theologian who addressed the morality of divination.</note> very beautifully refuted auguries, **lots** <term>lots</term> <note>original: "sortes." The practice of casting lots (like rolling dice or drawing straws) to determine the future or divine will.</note>, and other divinatory arts in that short treatise which he wrote *Concerning Lots* <note>original: "de sortibus." This refers to Aquinas's letter *De Sortibus ad Dominum Iacobum de Tonengo*.</note>. For he argues as follows: the **stars** have a greater connection with human actions—which proceed from our own intellect and will—than **auguries** do. Yet human actions do not proceed from the stars except in a contingent <note>Meaning "by chance" or "dependent on other factors," rather than being strictly predetermined.</note> and accidental manner; therefore, human actions cannot be predicted through auguries, which have only a contingent connection with those actions.

Furthermore, that human actions do not occur by necessity because of the stars themselves is testified by **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the most influential astronomer of antiquity, whose work was the standard for medieval science.</note>, who affirms that "the wise man overcomes the stars," both in his *Book of a Hundred Sayings* <note>original: "libro centum enuntiatorum," widely known as the *Centiloquium*, a collection of astrological aphorisms attributed to Ptolemy.</note> and in the introduction to his *Apotelesmata* <term>Apotelesmata</term> <note>Also known as the *Tetrabiblos*, Ptolemy's foundational work on the effects of the heavens on earthly affairs.</note>. Therefore, human actions do not necessarily follow from auguries, neither as if they were the **causes** of those actions, nor as if they were reliable **signs**.

<summary>This page presents a logical refutation of divination by citing Thomas Aquinas, who argued that since even the stars do not have a necessary or controlling influence over human free will, lesser omens like auguries and lots certainly cannot predict human behavior.</summary>

<keywords>Thomas Aquinas, Ptolemy, augury, astrology, free will, divination, Centiloquium, Tetrabiblos, lots, contingency</keywords>

### The argument of Favorinus and Pliny against auguries.

Favorinus <note>Favorinus of Arelate (c. 80–160 AD) was a skeptical philosopher and orator during the Roman Empire.</note> indeed argues, as recorded in the works of Gellius <note>Aulus Gellius (c. 125–180 AD) was a Roman author famous for his *Attic Nights*, a collection of notes on grammar, philosophy, and history.</note>, that trust should not be placed in **oracles** <term>oracles</term> <note>original: "divinaculis." Small shrines or methods of seeking divine guidance about the future.</note> in the following way: for, he says, they predict either adversity or prosperity. If they promise prosperity and they deceive you, you will become miserable by waiting in vain; if they promise happy things that are actually going to happen, the expectation will weary you while you are suspended in hope, and that hope will have already withered the fruit of your future joy. If they speak of adversity and they lie, you will become miserable by fearing in vain; if they do not lie, you would become miserable in your mind from that very moment, before you even become so by the event itself.

Furthermore, as Pliny <note>Likely Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), the Roman naturalist who often expressed skepticism toward magic and superstition.</note> says, it is the mark of neither a free nor a noble-minded man to be enslaved to the suspicions of prophecies, or to be troubled by the superstitions of diviners.

For this reason, it is also cautioned in the sacred histories by the Lord that "it is not for you to know the times or the moments which the Father has placed in his own power" <note>original: "non esse uestrum noscere tempora, uel momenta, quæ pater posuit in sua potestate." This is a quote from the New Testament, Acts 1:7.</note>. Nor is this a matter of injustice; for if it were necessary to know any future things for the public good of the human race, or for the private good of any person, these [would be granted] by divine...

# CONCERNING AUGURY

...must be sought in the sacred scriptures. **Natural signs**, however, are those which Physicians or natural Philosophers <term>natural philosopher: a scholar who studies the physical world and its laws, the historical precursor to the modern scientist</term> use to signify something of the future. These are called "natural" because they can be assigned either a human or a natural cause. 

A human cause is seen, for example, if there were a sudden flight of birds from a certain location; this could be an augury to indicate to us that ambushes <note>original: *insidias*</note> lie hidden in the place from which the birds suddenly took flight. The cause of this sign is attributed to our own human ingenuity <note>meaning we use logic to infer that someone must have startled the birds</note>.

A **Natural** cause, on the other hand, occurs when someone predicts approaching rain because a crow caws frequently. This can be divined <note>original: *auspicari*, a term derived from watching birds for omens</note> from a natural cause, since animals are moved by a certain natural instinct influenced by the celestial bodies. They are prompted by the condition of the air to recognize the seasons, as is necessary for their own natures, following that which is written in the eighth chapter of Jeremiah:

> The kite in the sky knows its appointed time, and the turtledove, the swallow, and the stork observe the time of their coming. <note>Jeremiah 8:7. The author uses this biblical quote to show that animal behavior is a reliable, God-given natural clock rather than a supernatural miracle.</note>

**Furthermore**, if a physician takes a double sneeze <note>original: *sternutamentum*</note> as an augury, and says...

...that a sick person is being freed from the danger of death, he can say this from a natural cause. For one sign can be a matter of chance, while more than two signs might be caused by the impulse of rheum <term>rheum: a flow of bodily fluids, often associated with discharge or inflammation</term>, but two signs together prove the strength of the vital power <note>original: *uirtutis*, referring to the body's natural internal strength to fight disease</note>. **In addition to these, for a sick person to return to their accustomed anger is accepted by physicians as a good augury in patients, for it presages that the sick are returning to their natural dispositions.**

**Furthermore, if a Physician takes a twitching <term>saltus: literally a "jump" or "leap," referring to an involuntary muscle contraction or palpitation</term> as an augury to signify the "accidents of the soul"** <note>emotions or mental states that affect the body</note>, such as Joy, Anger, or Sadness, and things of that kind, it is not outside of natural reason. For as Avicenna <note>a famous Persian physician and philosopher (c. 980–1037) whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe for centuries</note> says in Book III, Fen III, Treatise One, Chapter XVIII:

A twitching sometimes happens from "animal accidents" <note>disturbances of the "animal spirits" which were thought to control sensation and movement</note>, for the movement of the spirits <term>spirits: in medieval medicine, these were subtle substances that carried life force through the body</term> which exists in the passions of the soul sometimes converts humors <note>the four primary bodily fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile</note> into windiness <note>original: *uentositates*, referring to gas or internal vapors</note>, by which the jumping of a limb occurs. For this same reason, twitching also announces apoplexy <term>apoplexy: a sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, often a stroke</term>, and when it persists in the membrane covering the belly, it presages melancholy <term>melancholy: a condition of gloom or depression, then thought to be caused by an excess of black bile</term> and epilepsy. But when it persists in the face, it foretells a twisting <note>likely referring to facial paralysis or Bell's palsy</note>, and when it occurs in the hypochondria <term>hypochondria: the upper part of the abdomen, just below the ribs</term>, it presages an abscess <note>original: *apostema*</note>.

# ON AUGURIES

Therefore, if someone takes a tremor as an augury to signify these things, he will not be acting outside of natural reason. If even a **meteorologist** <term>meteorologicus: in the Renaissance, this referred to a student of all atmospheric and celestial phenomena, not just weather</term> takes "three suns" <note>This refers to a "sun dog" or parhelion, an optical phenomenon where bright spots appear on either side of the sun</note> as an augury to signify heavy rains, he does not act against natural reason. By a similar logic, he may accept as signs: three moons, a **burning shield** appearing in the sky, the clashing of arms, or the sound of trumpets being heard. He might even consider reports of it "raining milk and blood," as occurred during the consulship of **Marcus Atilius and Caius Porcius** <note>Consuls in 115–114 BCE; such "prodigies" were regularly recorded in Roman official annals</note>.

Likewise, it is said to have rained meat during the consulship of **Lucius Volumnius and Servius Sulpicius**. Again, it reportedly rained iron in the land of the Lucanians <note>an area in Southern Italy</note> shortly before **Marcus Crassus** was killed by the Parthians <note>Crassus, a member of the First Triumvirate, was famously defeated and killed at the Battle of Carrhae in 53 BCE</note>. If a meteorologist accepts these events as auguries to signify natural effects, he is not in error, since they can have natural causes.

Furthermore, if someone takes the sight of **vultures** flying over an army for two or three days as an augury to signify the slaughter of that army, they are not mistaken—if it is true what **Pliny** says in Book 10: *Vultures sense corpses that are to be, as well as those already present.* <note>Pliny the Elder, *Natural History*, 10.19. The author suggests vultures have a natural biological "scent" for impending death, making their presence a rational sign rather than a magical one</note>.

## BOOK II.

If someone takes a **sneeze** as an augury to signify the truth of what they are saying, or the evasion of an imminent danger, they are predicting the truth superstitiously, frivolously, and by mere chance. For augurs take a sneeze as an omen to signify many things, as has been seen. Especially, as *Albertus* <note>original: "Albertus"; likely referring to Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), the medieval scholar known for his works on natural philosophy</note> says, it signifies two things: namely, the truth of the statement during which the sneeze occurred, and the escape from a prepared danger—for instance, when someone is facing an ambush by another—especially if they sneeze twice. 

For a double sneeze signifies a greatness of **power** <term>uirtus: in this context, it refers to an internal force or vital energy, often linked to the influence of the stars</term>, which would not exist unless the person who sneezes twice were assisted by celestial powers. This is much like how a **critical day** <term>critica dies: in medieval medicine, specific days during an illness when a patient was expected to either recover or decline, often believed to be governed by the moon</term> would not occur unless it were reinforced by celestial power. With the celestial power fostering it, the word is fulfilled, it matches the reality, and thus it will be true. 

By a similar logic, it is an omen for signifying the escape from a prepared danger, for he who is strengthened by the powers of the heavens cannot be overcome by earthly and **sublunary** <note>The region below the moon (Earth), which was thought to be subject to change and decay, unlike the eternal heavens</note> things. But I myself judge that everything which a sneeze signifies...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 515 regarding the interpretation of sneezing as an omen. -># ON AUGURIES<-

...aside from the escape of the vulnerable, it is frivolous, superstitious, and accidental. It is **accidental** indeed, since it happens by chance that someone, by sneezing, escapes a danger prepared through ambush; this is because it is beyond the intention of the one sneezing and of those who prepared the ambush. 

It is truly **frivolous**, because if anyone accepts a sneeze as signifying the truth of a statement or the escape from an imminent danger, they are using a thing to signify something to which the inherent power of that thing does not extend. For the power of a sneeze cannot manifest itself for such purposes. Such a thing is called "frivolous," as **Augustine** <note>original: "Augustinus"; Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD). In his work *De Doctrina Christiana* (On Christian Doctrine), he argues that assigning meaning to natural events like sneezing is a form of "frivolous" superstition.</note> says in Book II of *On Christian Doctrine*. 

Finally, it is **superstitious**, because it is observed through a persistent, inept, and false religion. For as **Gellius** <note>original: "Gellius"; Aulus Gellius (c. 125 – after 180 AD), a Roman author whose work *Attic Nights* discusses the distinction between proper religion and "inept" superstition.</note> says, every persistent and inept religion can be called a superstition. For this reason, it is said in **Deuteronomy 18**: 

> There shall not be found among you anyone who purifies his son or daughter by leading them through fire, or who consults soothsayers, or observes dreams and auguries, nor shall there be any sorcerer or charmer, nor shall he consult **pythons** <term>Phitones: derived from the Greek "Python," referring to the serpent of Delphi. In medieval and biblical Latin, it refers to mediums, ventriloquists, or those who consult spirits of the dead</term>, nor diviners, nor those who...

# BOOK II.
h 3

...the truth from the dead <note>original: "rat à mortuis ueritatem"; this completes the sentence from the previous page, likely "quaerat à mortuis ueritatem," referring to the prohibition against necromancy.</note>. By a similar logic, we must speak of the hopping, the flights, and the songs of birds; for when these are taken to signify something beyond their own natural powers, they are interpreted superstitiously, frivolously, and as a matter of chance. From these points, it is clear which auguries <term>auguries: the practice of interpreting omens from the behavior of birds or other natural phenomena</term> should be believed and which should not. For if someone believes in divine and natural signs, they are neither vain, nor frivolous, nor superstitious. But he who believes in auguries that signify nothing except by chance, this man is vain, superstitious, and frivolous.

### *Explanation of the true cause of superstitious auguries.*

From this, it is evident that the true cause of superstitious auguries is the demon <note>original: "daemonem"; in the medieval and Renaissance view, demons were believed to be the hidden agents behind "lucky" or "unlucky" signs to lead people away from God.</note>. As **Augustine** <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD).</note> says in the second book of *On Christian Doctrine*: "All practitioners of this kind of either frivolous or harmful superstition—born from a certain pestilential society of men and demons, established as if by a pact of faithless and deceitful friendship—must be utterly rejected and avoided by the Christian." <note>original: "Omnes artifices... fugienda christiano"; a famous passage from Augustine's De Doctrina Christiana, arguing that interest in omens is a "contract" with evil spirits.</note> For demons, in such matters, effectively... 

<summary>This page distinguishes between legitimate natural signs and superstitious auguries, such as bird behavior, which it labels as "frivolous" and "accidental." It concludes by citing Saint Augustine to argue that all such superstitions are the result of a deceitful spiritual pact between humans and demons.</summary>

<keywords>augury, superstition, demons, Saint Augustine, bird omens, divination, On Christian Doctrine, omens</keywords>

> Blessed is the man whose hope is in the name of the Lord, and who has not looked upon vanities and false madness. <note>original: "Beatus uir, cuius est nomen domini spes eius, & non respexit in uanitates, et insanias falsas"; Psalm 40:4 (Vulgate 39:5).</note>

Thus far concerning the true cause of auguries.

---

### *Refutation of the cause assigned by Pliny, through which he gave credit to auguries.*

Furthermore, it is clear from these points that our intention—by which we refer an augury to either good or evil—is not the cause for auguries to signify the truth, as Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), the Roman author who suggested in his *Natural History* that omens only have power if the observer accepts them.</note> believed. For if that were so, then imagination would also be the cause of health or harm, which is false. For according to the Peripatetics <term>Peripatetics: followers of Aristotle’s school of philosophy, known for their focus on natural observation and physical causality</term>, a power or virtue belonging to one body cannot be the true cause of an effect in another body, unless perhaps as a stimulating cause. This is evident in the case of horses: when one urinates, the other urinates as well. For the imagination by which one imagines the onset of a fever and a fever follows is not its cause, except by accident. Yet the imagination by which a person, while walking along a narrow beam, imagines falling and then does fall, is indeed the cause of that fall; for that fall [itself]... 

<meta>catchword: that [it] proceeds</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a theological warning against demonic deception in signs before refuting Pliny the Elder's theory that human intention gives omens their power. The author uses Peripatetic philosophy and physical examples (like the sympathetic behavior of horses) to argue that imagination cannot be a primary cause of external events.</summary>

BOOK II.

h 4

...causally <note>The Latin text completes the word started on the previous page: "causale" (causally).</note> proceeds from fear, the cause of which is the imagination of a fall. But fear is something within us. Since, therefore, our power of imagination has no power over birds, nor over beasts, nor over auguries of that kind, it follows that it is not pertinent to auguries.

### *Refutation of the reasoning of Plutarch and the younger philosophers, by which they granted credibility to auguries.*

For the same reason, the vanity of the reasoning by which Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46–120 AD) was a Greek philosopher and priest who frequently wrote on the religious significance of omens and the divine.</note> granted credibility to auguries is evident. Since "right" and "left" are merely relative positions—by which an augury is called right or left not based on the augury itself, but based on us—it follows that this reasoning is vain, trifling, and superstitious. For there is no inherent power in these relative positions that extends to such meanings, since they are neither the causes nor the effects of such things. Furthermore, that which exists only in relation to a living being is not "right" or "left" in its own essence; and what is not right or left in itself cannot be inherently good or evil. Therefore, no augury is good or evil except by accident <term>by accident: in philosophy, something that happens by chance or as a non-essential property, rather than by a direct causal link</term> and at random, insofar as it happens

<vocab>Plutarch, augury, superstition, relations, right, left, fear, imagination</vocab>

<summary>This page refutes Plutarch’s belief in omens by arguing that "right" and "left" are subjective human perspectives rather than inherent qualities of nature. It concludes that because these directions are relative to the observer, they cannot possess any real power to signal good or evil.</summary>
