# CONCERNING AUGURIES

or by an omen passing from the right to the left, so that I may complete my business; because we have observed the opposite most often, for the same reason the case of the younger philosophers is refuted, as is clear to anyone considering it. <note>The author continues from the previous page, arguing that because the "direction" of an omen depends entirely on where a person is standing, it cannot have a fixed meaning.</note>

->### *Refutation of the reasoning of those who grant credibility to auguries based on the positions of the heavens.*<-

For the same reason, the case of those who grant credibility to auguries based on the position of the sky is refuted. For they claim that auguries are good when they proceed toward that same part of the heaven in which the beneficent stars <term>beneficent stars: in traditional astrology, planets like Jupiter and Venus, which were believed to bring good fortune</term> are flourishing, and bad which proceed toward that same part in which the malefic stars <term>malefic stars: planets like Saturn and Mars, traditionally associated with hardship or misfortune</term> have power. If this were so, then everything that moved toward that part in which the beneficent stars have power would be good, and truly everything moving toward the part where the malefic stars are would be bad. 

But we have often observed the opposite. Indeed, we have very often seen the beneficent stars, by their own proper motions, wander into parts of the sky where previously the malefic stars were located, and also the reverse. And yet, we have observed that auguries which ought to be good by the power of the heavens were in fact bad, and those which ought to be bad by the virtue of the heavens, we have seen to be good. This occurs for no other reason than because auguries 

<meta>catchword: have no (nullam)</meta>

<summary>The author refutes the astrological justification for augury, arguing that the shifting positions of "good" and "bad" planets prove that a bird's direction of flight has no inherent spiritual or predictive meaning.</summary>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

...they have no significance in themselves, except by chance and accident. From these same points, the error of those who place faith in auguries because of the stars is evident—specifically those stars which rule the ninth house <term>ninth house: in astrology, the "House of God," traditionally associated with religion, philosophy, and divination</term> at the time of the augury. For as the most holy doctor <note>likely referring to St. Thomas Aquinas or St. Augustine, who both argued against the power of stars over human free will</note> rightly thinks: if the stars cannot act upon our actions, then neither can auguries act upon our actions, even if they were directed by those stars. For there cannot be a greater power in the arrow than there was in the one who shot it. <note>original: "Non enim maior uirtus eſſe poterit in ſagitta q̃ fuerit in proiiciente." This is a common medieval philosophical analogy: an effect (the arrow) cannot have more power or agency than its cause (the archer).</note> That the stars indeed cannot act upon our human actions must be assumed for now, since we have proven it elsewhere.

->*Refutation of the cause assigned by Themistius, Theophrastus, Hermes, Varro, and others.*<-

Furthermore, those who have said that faith should be granted to auguries because they are moved by God—if they are speaking of superstitious, trifling, and reckless auguries—they are to be laughed at. By similar reasoning, those who say that auguries are moved by good spirits <note>original: "bonis geniis"</note>, who are called intelligences or angels, are to be refuted; for nothing superstitious, nothing trifling, and nothing vain can originate from the One who is simply and purely good. But the followers of Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro was an ancient Roman scholar who wrote extensively on religion and divine signs</note>, and others...

<meta>catchword: who say</meta>

<summary>The author argues that stars and celestial 'houses' have no power over human will, meaning they cannot validate the practice of augury. He further contends that neither God nor angels would use trivial or superstitious signs to communicate with humanity.</summary>

<page-num>122</page-num>

### ON AUGURIES

Those who say that auguries are set in motion by the Moon alone, or by a spirit <note>original: "dæmone"</note> through the favorable intervention of the Moon, are to be entirely rejected <note>original: "explodendi," a term meaning to hiss off a stage or drive out</note>. For if the Moon alone, as it runs through the signs of the zodiac, were the moving cause, it would happen that both good and evil would be signified at the same time and to the same person. For example, if a wild beast were to enter a house while the Moon was held in Aries <term>Aries: the first sign of the zodiac, traditionally associated with the element of fire and a forceful, sometimes contentious nature</term>, it would portend evil, because it would presage strife and slander, which are evils. But if a sound were heard in that same house while the Moon was in Aries, it would presage something good, specifically joy and gladness. Yet, since both of these events could happen at the very same time while the Moon occupies that same sign of the heaven, it would follow that both a good and an evil augury occurred simultaneously—which I consider to be ridiculous. 

Furthermore, if a good spirit <note>original: "Dæmon"</note> moves the auguries through the intervention of the Moon—as the younger thinkers <note>likely referring to contemporary Neoplatonist philosophers who sought to find a "natural" or "spiritual" basis for magic and divination</note> believe—this seems to be far more superstitious, trifling, and vain. For since the Moon and a good spirit <note>original: "genius bonus"</note> are most orderly things, entirely free from all trifling, superstition, and chance, they do not seem likely to move things that signify nothing of the truth, unless they are being understood through the lens of superstition.

<meta>catchword: To these</meta>

<summary>The author uses logical deduction to argue that the Moon and spiritual "genii" cannot be the causes of auguries, noting that if they were, contradictory omens would occur simultaneously.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Moon, Aries, omens, daemon, genius bonus, superstition, divination, logic</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page concludes the theological and philosophical refutation of the causes of augury, transitioning from the argument that celestial bodies control human fate to a summary of the first book's contents. The catchword "De rebus" indicates that the following page (likely the start of Book II proper) will begin a discussion "Concerning things..."</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>

->**BOOK II.**<-

In addition to these points, our actions do not originate from the Moon, nor from the guiding spirit <note>original: "genio"; in Roman belief, the *genius* was a protective spirit assigned to a person at birth</note> itself, since such things cannot exert power over our free will. It is nevertheless true that demons <note>original: "daemones"; the author uses this in the Christian sense of deceptive spirits</note>, having observed certain stars and especially the Moon, join together to perform many things. They do this for one purpose: to lead humanity into the error of believing that there is some divine power <note>original: "numen"</note> residing in the stars. This concludes the discussion on auguries.

Indeed, you are so devoted to the study of all things that you wish to possess them all; you search for them from every direction, so that some works, longed for over many years, are now finally read thanks to your efforts. # THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO
## OF THE NILE
### *Translated from the Greek by Bernardino Trebatio*
### *of Vicenza*

->*How they signify Eternity.*<- <note>original: "Aeuum" (Aevum). This term refers to an "Age," a vast span of time, or "Eternity."</note>

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut drop cap 'A' featuring intricate floral and foliate patterns within a square border.</image-desc>When signifying **Eternity**, they depict the **Sun** and the **Moon**, because these planets are the elements of time. Wishing to represent **Eternity** in another way, they paint a **Serpent**, covering its tail with the rest of its body <note>This is a description of the Ouroboros, the ancient symbol of a snake devouring its own tail to symbolize the cyclical nature of time and the universe.</note>; the Egyptians call it the **Uraeus**, which is to say the **Basilisk**. <term>Uraeus</term> <note>original: "Vreum." From the Ancient Greek "ouraios," meaning "of the tail." It refers to the stylized, upright form of an Egyptian cobra, used as a symbol of sovereignty, royalty, and divine authority.</note> Once they have fashioned this serpent in gold, they place it around the statues of the gods. The Egyptians say that **Eternity** is signified by this animal because, while there are three kinds of serpents, the others indeed die, but this one alone is **immortal**. Furthermore, it kills any other living creature simply by breathing on it, even without a bite. Therefore, since it seems to possess the power of life and death, for this very reason they place it upon the heads of the gods.

<summary>This opening page of Horapollo’s treatise explains the symbolic representation of eternity through celestial bodies and the immortal Uraeus serpent, or basilisk, which adorns the heads of Egyptian deities.</summary>

THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO

### *How they signify the World.*

<margin>Chapter 2</margin>When wishing to represent the **World**, they paint a **Serpent** devouring its own tail <note>This is the Ouroboros, a symbol of the cyclical nature of the universe where creation and destruction are part of the same process.</note>, adorned with various scales, by which they represent the stars of the **World**. Indeed, this animal is very heavy in proportion to its size, just like the earth; it is also slippery, and in this respect similar to water; and every single year it sheds its skin along with its old age <note>The Latin "senio" implies not just old skin, but the frailty of age itself.</note>. According to this logic, the annual cycle in the world also grows young again as it undergoes change. Moreover, the fact that it uses its own body for food signifies that whatever things are generated in the world by divine providence, all these suffer a reduction back into the same <note>This reflects the Stoic and Neoplatonic idea that the universe is a self-sustaining system that consumes its own waste to generate new life.</note>.

### *How they signify the Year.*

Wishing to signify the **Year**, they paint **Isis**, that is, a woman; by this same image, they also signify the goddess. Among the Egyptians, **Isis** is a star, called **Sothis** in their language, and **Astromion** <term>Astromion</term> <note>original: "Astromion." This refers to the "Dog Star" or Sirius. In Greek, it is often called *Seirios*.</note> in the language of the Greeks. This star appears to rule over the other stars, appearing sometimes larger, sometimes smaller, and at one time indeed brighter, at other times...

...otherwise. Furthermore, at its rising <note>This refers to the heliacal rising of the star Sirius, which signaled the start of the Nile's flood.</note>, all things that will happen in the coming year are usually foreseen. For this reason, they quite rightly called the Year **Isis**. <margin>The Palm Tree</margin> And describing the year in another way, they paint a **Palm tree**, because this tree alone, contrary to the nature of all others, puts forth one branch at each rising of the **Moon**, so that a complete year is finished in twelve branches.

### *How they signify a Month.*

When writing a **Month**, they paint a **branch**, or an **inverted Moon**. They use the **branch** indeed for the same reason already mentioned regarding the palm tree. They use the **inverted Moon**, however, because as they say, when it first rises, fifteen parts remain until it appears with its horns stretched toward the upper part. But when it is hidden, having completed the count of thirty days, it turns its horns toward the lower part.

### *How they signify the following Year.*

When signifying the **following Year**, they describe a **fourth part of a field** <term>arura</term> <note>original: "aruum." The Greek source uses "aroura," a standard Egyptian land measure of 100 square cubits.</note>. Now, a field is a measure of land of **one hundred cubits**; thus, when they wish to say "year,"

...they say "a fourth," because as they report, from one rising of the star which they call **Sothis** <note>original: "Sothidem." This is the star Sirius, whose heliacal rising was the focal point of the Egyptian calendar.</note> to the next rising, there is an interval of a fourth part of a day. They also say that the solar year consists of three hundred and sixty-five days. Because of this, every fourth year the **Egyptians** add an extra day; for a fourth part taken four times completes a full day.

### *What they signify when they draw an*
### *Eagle.*

When they wish to signify a **god**, or **height**, or **lowness** <note>original: "depressio." This likely refers to the bird's ability to descend rapidly or perhaps a concept of "depth."</note>, or **excellence**, or **blood**, or **victory**, they paint an **Eagle**. <note>While the Latin text says *Aquila* (Eagle), the original Egyptian hieroglyphs and the Greek source text refer to a Hawk or Falcon, the symbol of Horus.</note> They use it for a **god**, because this bird is prolific and long-lived. Furthermore, it appears to be an image of the **Sun**, since, contrary to the nature of all other winged creatures, it keeps its eyes fixed intently upon the solar rays. For this reason, physicians even use a herb named after the eagle <note>This refers to *hierakion* or "Hawkweed," which ancient naturalists believed hawks consumed to sharpen their vision.</note> for the healing of eyes; and they sometimes depict the **Sun**—as the master of sight—in the form of an **Eagle**. They use it for **height**, because while other birds move at an angle when they wish to fly upward, since they cannot fly straight up, the eagle alone...

BOOK I. 135

the **Eagle** <note>As noted previously, while the Latin text says *Aquila* (Eagle), the Egyptian context refers to the Hawk or Falcon, specifically the symbol of the god Horus.</note> is carried straight up into the heights. They use it to signify **lowness** <note>original: "Depressionem." In this context, it refers to a vertical descent or the bird's ability to drop straight down from the sky.</note>, because in the same way that other birds fly toward the earth at an angle, the Eagle alone descends straight down. It signifies **excellence**, because the Eagle is seen to surpass all other winged creatures. It signifies **blood**, because, as they say, the Eagle does not drink water, but blood. It signifies **victory**, because it is seen to overcome all other birds. For if, during a fight, it feels itself being defeated, it throws itself onto its back and directs its talons upward—with its wings and back turned toward the ground—and in this manner it continues to fight. The enemy bird, however, being unable to do this, is easily put to flight.

->How they indicate the Soul.<-

The **Eagle** is also used to represent the **soul**, based on an interpretation of its name. Among the **Egyptians**, the soul is called **Baieth** <note>original: "Baieth." This is a famous piece of ancient folk-etymology. The Egyptian word *Ba* refers to a component of the soul often depicted as a bird with a human head. The author connects it here to the word for heart.</note>. This name, when divided, signifies "soul" and "heart"; for *bai* <note>original: "bai"</note> is the soul, and *eth* <note>original: "eth"</note> is the heart. Moreover, according to the Egyptians, the heart is the "enclosure" or "circuit" of the soul, so that the name itself sounds like "a soul with a heart." For this reason, because the Eagle is of the same nature as the soul, it generally does not drink water, but blood—upon which the soul is also nourished. <note>This reflects an ancient belief, common in many Mediterranean cultures, that the soul or life-force resided in or was sustained by blood.</note>

### How they represent Mars 
### & Venus.

To represent **Mars** <note>original: "Martem." The Roman god of war, here representing the Greek Ares or an Egyptian equivalent associated with virility.</note> and **Venus** <note>original: "Venerem." The Roman goddess of love, representing Aphrodite or an Egyptian equivalent associated with desire and fertility.</note>, they paint two **eagles** <note>As noted on previous pages, the text uses "Eagle" (Aquila) to refer to the Egyptian Hawk or Falcon.</note>: a male, whom they compare to Mars, and a female, whom they compare to Venus. This is because other animals do not always wish to submit to the male for every act of mating, but the female Eagle always does. For even if she has been coupled with thirty times in a single day, after she has risen, if the male calls, she runs to him again. Therefore, because she is so greatly inclined toward **Venus** <note>In this context, meaning the act of love or sexual desire.</note>, the Egyptians call her Venus. For this reason, they also dedicated the Eagle to the **Sun**, because, imitating the Sun, it completes the number thirty <note>original: "ter denum numerum." This likely refers to the thirty days of the solar month.</note> in the act of mating. 

Alternatively, when writing of Mars and Venus, they paint twin **crows** <note>original: "cornices." Crows or ravens were celebrated in antiquity as symbols of marital fidelity.</note>, a male and a female. This is because this animal lays two eggs, from which a male and a female must be born. If it should happen—which occurs only rarely—that two males or two females are born, the males, once paired with females, do not mate with any other crow. Likewise, the female will not mate with another male; instead, if they are separated, they live alone. Therefore, to a single crow...

### *How they represent Marriage.*

When they wish to signify **marriage**, they paint twin **crows**, for the reasons mentioned above <note>Referring to the crow's reputation for lifelong monogamy and fidelity.</note>.

### *How they represent an Only-Begotten.*

To signify an **Only-Begotten** <note>original: "Vnigenitum." While this term has Christian theological weight, here it refers to a being that is the "only one of its kind" or self-produced.</note>, or **generation**, or a **father**, or the **world**, or a **man**, they paint a **Scarab** <note>original: "Scarabeum." The dung beetle was a potent Egyptian symbol for the god Khepri, representing the self-creating sun.</note>. They use it for the "only-begotten" because this animal is self-generated, not conceived by a female. For its generation is unique, as follows:

<margin>The generation of the Scarab.</margin>
When the male wishes to procreate, he finds the dung of cattle and fashions a small, round ball from it, in the likeness of the heavens. Pushing it with his hind legs, he rolls it from east to west, while he himself faces toward the rising **Sun**, until the round ball has taken on the form of the world. For the Sun is carried from east to west, but the course of the stars is carried by a contrary motion from west to east. Therefore, after burying this ball, he leaves it for twenty-eight days. <note>The twenty-eight days correspond to the lunar cycle, reflecting the ancient belief that biology mirrored celestial movements.</note>

There are, moreover, three forms of **Scarabs**. They become elongated in the morning at the rising of the Sun, they become round around midday, and they appear dimmed as the Sun sets. Hence, in the **City of the Sun** <note>original: "urbe Solis." Heliopolis, the center of Egyptian solar worship.</note>, the statue of the god is cat-shaped. ### *What they signify by writing a Vulture.*

<margin>The generation of Vultures.</margin>
When writing <note>In the sense of "drawing" or "carving" a hieroglyph.</note> a **Mother**, or **sight**, or a **boundary**, or **foreknowledge**, or a **year**, or **mercy**, or **Pallas** <note>The Greek Athena; in an Egyptian context, this likely refers to Neith.</note>, or **Juno** <note>The Roman Hera; in an Egyptian context, this refers to the goddess Mut, whose name is written with the vulture sign.</note>, or **two drachmas**, they paint a **Vulture**. And if ever the food needed to nourish the offspring is lacking, so that they do not perish from hunger, it wounds its own thigh and offers its own blood to be sucked. <note>original: "sanguinem sugendum præbet." This myth of self-sacrifice is more commonly associated with the pelican in later medieval bestiaries, but Horapollo attributes it here to the vulture.</note> They also use it to signify **Pallas and Juno**, because it seems to the Egyptians that **Pallas** <note>In the Egyptian context, this likely refers to the goddess Neith.</note> has claimed the upper hemisphere of the sky for herself, while **Juno** <note>Likely referring to the goddess Mut or Hathor.</note> has claimed the lower. For this reason, they also think it absurd that the sky should be designated by a masculine gender, since the generation of the **Sun**, the **Moon**, and the rest of the stars is accomplished through feminine labor. <note>In Latin, the word for sky, *cœlum*, is neuter, but the author argues for a feminine conception of the cosmos based on Egyptian theology.</note> And the race of **Vultures**, as has been said, is entirely female; for this reason, they assign the Vulture as the ruler <note>original: "regem" (king/ruler). Despite the bird being female, the author uses the masculine title of 'king' to denote its sovereignty over other birds.</note> of all other birds of this sex. Indeed, with this Vulture—to avoid drawing the discourse out too long—they designate any goddess. Furthermore, when they wish to signify a **Mother**, they paint a Vulture, for a mother is of the female sex. They also represent **two drachmas** <note>A unit of currency or weight; the vulture symbol was used to denote the number two in certain contexts.</note> by a painted Vulture, because among the 

->Egyptians<-

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of the vulture as a symbol for motherhood and feminine divinity. It concludes the vulture section by explaining its numerical value before moving on to the symbolism of stars and baboons.</meta>

->HORAPOLLO’S HIEROGLYPHICS<-

Among the Egyptians, the unit is equal to two drachmas <note>original: "dragmae." The drachma was a unit of weight and currency. Here, the author notes a specific Egyptian accounting convention where the standard unit was composed of two parts.</note>. Since the unit is the principle <note>original: "principium" (the beginning or fundamental source).</note> of all numbers, they quite rightly write a **Vulture** when they wish to signify **two drachmas**, because the vulture appears to be both a mother and a principle, just as the unit is.

### *What they signify by writing a Star.*

To signify **God**, or **fate**, or the **number five**, they paint a **star**. They use it for **God** because all the movement of the stars and the entire world is accomplished by divine providence, for it seems that nothing at all can exist without God. They use it for **Fate**, because fate is established by the movement of the stars. They use it for the **number five**, because although there are many stars in the heavens, only five of them are in motion <note>original: "quinque sola mota." This refers to the five "wandering stars" or planets known to the ancients: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn. The Sun and Moon were often categorized separately.</note> and complete the arrangement of the entire world.

### *What they signify by writing a Baboon.*

When they wish to signify the **Moon**, or the **habitable world**, or **letters**, or **anger**, or **swimming**, they paint a **baboon** <term>Cynocephalus</term> <note>Literally "dog-head." In Egyptian iconography, this refers to the hamadryas baboon, which was sacred to Thoth, the god of wisdom and the moon.</note>. For the **Moon**, indeed—

# LIBER I.

...indeed, because this animal is affected in various ways according to the course of the Moon. For when the Moon is joined to the Sun and becomes invisible, the male **baboon** <term>Cynocephalus</term> <note>Literally "dog-head." To the ancients, the hamadryas baboon's muzzle resembled that of a dog.</note> neither sees nor eats; he is gripped by sorrow, with his eyes cast down toward the earth as if he were mourning the abduction of the Moon. The female, moreover, in addition to being unable to see and being affected in the same way as the male, suffers a flow of blood from her private parts. For this reason, baboons are even to this day nourished in the sacred temples, so that through them the conjunction <note>The "conjunction" refers to the New Moon, when the Moon and Sun are at the same celestial longitude and the Moon is not visible from Earth.</note> of the Sun and Moon may be clearly observed. 

They use the baboon to signify the **Inhabited World** because they say there are seventy-two zones <note>original: "climata mundi." In ancient geography, these were latitudinal strips of the earth.</note> of the world. They claim that these animals, when carefully raised in the temples, do not die all at once on a single day like other animals; rather, each day a specific portion of the animal dies and is buried by the priests, while the rest of the body retains its original nature. This continues until the seventy-second day, at which point the entire creature finally dies. 

They also use it to signify **letters**, because among the Egyptians, there is a breed of baboons that knows how to write. Therefore, as soon as a baboon is first brought into the temple, the priest places a writing tablet and a 

->stylus<- <note>original: "stilum." A pointed tool used for writing on wax or clay tablets.</note>

->*How they signify the New Moon.*<-

When they wish to represent the **New Moon**, they once again paint a baboon standing with its forepaws raised toward heaven, wearing a royal ornament <note>This likely refers to the "pschent" crown or the lunar disk and crescent often seen on Thoth-associated animals in Egyptian art.</note> upon its head. They depict the baboon in this posture when the New Moon appears, as if it were giving thanks to the god, because the Moon, having moved past the Sun, now shines with its own light and no longer has its light blocked by the Sun. <note>The text here reflects an ancient understanding of the lunar cycle where the "conjunction" with the sun was seen as a period of obscured light.</note>

->*How they signify the equinoxes.*<-

Wishing to signify the **equinoxes**, which occur twice a year, they paint a seated baboon. For this animal, at the time of the equinox, during each

### BOOK I.

From the Greek for "dog-headed."</note> on water-clocks <term>hydrologies</term> <note>original: "hydrologiis." This refers to the clepsydra or water-clock, used in antiquity to measure time by the flow of water.</note>, and they make the water flow out from its member. Because, as was said before, it signifies the twelve hours by its urinating. However, lest the opening through which the water exits into the clock be too wide or too narrow—for either would hinder the calculation of the hours—they fashion an iron pipe for this purpose according to the thickness of the tip of the animal's hairless tail. And it pleased them to do this not without reason, just as in other matters; and because also during the **equinoxes**, it alone among all animals barks twelve times in the day, once for each hour.

->*How they signify Courage, Anger, or*<-
->*Fury.*<-

Wishing to signify **Courage**, **Anger**, or **Fury**, they paint a **Lion**. This animal has a large head, and the pupils of its eyes are fiery; its body is rounded, and its hair is like rays, in imitation of the **Sun**. <note>The lion's mane was frequently compared to the rays of the sun in ancient solar symbolism.</note> Wherefore they also paint the **Lion** beneath the throne of the **Sun**, demonstrating its similarity to the Sun itself.

<header>HORAPOLLO’S HIEROGLYPHICS</header>

### *How they signify Fortitude.*

To signify **Fortitude** <note>original: "Fortitudinem." This term encompasses both physical strength and moral courage.</note>, they depict the front parts of a **Lion**, because these limbs are broader and stronger than the rest of its body.

### *How they signify a Vigilant person.*

When writing of a **vigilant person** or a **guardian**, they paint the head of a **Lion**. This is because the Lion, when it is awake, has its eyes closed, but when it sleeps, it keeps them open; this open-eyed sleep is considered a true sign of vigilance. For this reason, they quite rightly place statues of Lions at the gates of sacred enclosures <note>original: "sacrorum claustra." Referring to the entrances of temples or holy precincts, often guarded by sphinxes or lion figures.</note> to serve as guardians.

### *How they signify a Fear-inspiring man.*

To signify a man who inspires fear, they use the same sign. Since this animal is the strongest among all others, it strikes terror into everyone it encounters.

### *How they signify the inundation of the Nile.*

To signify the flooding of the **Nile**—for they call the Nile **Num**, which in our tongue means "new" <note>original: "Num... novum." The author is likely connecting the Egyptian name for the primordial waters (Nu/Nun) or a name for the Nile to the Latin/Greek word for "new," suggesting the flood brings a "new" year or "new" fertility.</note>—they sometimes paint a **Lion**, and sometimes **three water-jars** <term>hydrias</term> <note>original: "hydrias." These are large ceramic water vessels or pitchers.</note>. They use the **Lion** because the Sun causes the greatest floods when it enters the constellation of **Leo** <note>The Nile's annual flood traditionally began around the summer solstice, when the sun moved into the zodiac sign of Leo.</note>. They use **three jars**, and neither more nor fewer, because the cause of the 
->flooding<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the causes of the Nile’s annual flooding, concluding the astronomical and geographical reasons before moving into specific hieroglyphic symbols for the land of Egypt and the character of a homebody.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...of the flooding is assigned. One is the Egyptian land itself, which generates water from within itself. Another is the ocean, for the ocean flows back during the time of the flooding through the **Nile**. The third cause is the rains, which are very frequent during the time of the flood in the southern parts of **Ethiopia**. That **Egypt** generates water from itself can be understood in this way: for in the other regions <note>original: "climatibus." Referring to the ancient geographical concept of "climata" or latitudinal zones.</note> of the world, floods occur in the winter time due to continuous and excessive rains, but **Egypt** alone, situated in the middle of the earth like the pupil in an eye, produces the **Nile** floods for itself in the summer.

->### *How they write Egypt.*<-

When writing "Egypt," they paint a burning incense-burner <term>acerram</term> <note>original: "acerram." A vessel or box used for burning incense in sacrifices.</note>, and above it a **heart**, indicating that just as the heart of a jealous man <note>original: "Zelotypi." One who burns with zeal or jealousy.</note> is continually burning, so too does **Egypt**, because of its heat, continually bring forth the living creatures that are found there.

->### *How they signify a man who does not travel abroad.*<-

To signify a man who does not travel abroad <note>original: "non peregre proficiscentem." Someone who never leaves his home country.</note>, they paint the head of a **Donkey**, because he neither hears the accounts of history <note>original: "historias." This can mean both history and stories of the world.</note> nor takes notice of those who have traveled.

<meta>This page continues the sequence of hieroglyphic symbols from the previous page, moving from the symbol for a homebody (a donkey) to symbols representing protection, embryonic life, and speech.</meta>

<header>HORAPOLLO'S HIEROGLYPHICS</header>

### *How they signify Protection.*

When writing "Protection" <note>original: "Tutellam." This refers to guardianship or a protective watch.</note>, they paint two human heads, one of a male looking inward, and the other of a female looking outward; for they say that in this way no demon can invade, since even without letters <note>meaning: even without written charms or spells.</note>, they guard themselves with two heads.

### *How they signify an Embryo, that is, an imperfect human being.*

To signify an "Embryo" <note>original: "Embrionem."</note>, meaning an imperfect human being, they paint a **Frog**, because its generation is completed from the mud of the river. Whence it is possible at times to see a half-formed **Frog**, in one part a living animal, but in the other part merely something of the earth, such that if the river fails, the creature itself fails as well.

### *How they signify Speech.*

When writing "Speech" <note>original: "Orationem." This term can mean speech, discourse, or prayer.</note>, they paint a tongue and a wounded eye: giving the primary parts of discourse to the tongue, and the secondary to the eyes; for the speech of a perfect soul consists in this, when it is confirmed by its own movement <note>interpreted as: when the eyes reflect the soul's movement in harmony with the tongue.</note>. Alternatively, to signify speech, they paint a tongue and a hand underneath it, appearing to attribute the first parts of speech to the tongue, but the second parts to the hand, as if the hand were fulfilling the desires of the tongue.

<header>BOOK I.</header>

### *How they signify Silence.*

When signifying "Silence" <note>original: "Taciturnitatem."</note>, they write the number **1,095**. This is the number of days in three years, calculating the year at 365 days. Since an infant does not speak within this time, it signifies that even if the child possesses a tongue, they lack a voice <note>The author reflects an ancient belief that human speech is only truly established after the third year of life.</note>.

### *How they signify something open.*

To signify "Something Open," they paint a **Hare**, for this animal always has its eyes open <note>Ancient naturalists believed that hares slept with their eyes open, making them symbols of alertness or perpetual "openness."</note>.

### *How they signify a Resounding Voice.*

When they want to signify a "Resounding Voice" <note>original: "Vocem remotam." Literally a "distant voice," referring to a sound that carries across a great distance.</note>, which the Egyptians call *Vae*, they write the "voice of the air"—that is, **Thunder**. Nothing sounds deeper or more forceful than thunder.

### *How they signify Pleasure.*

When signifying "Pleasure" <note>original: "Voluptatem."</note>, they write the number **16**. They do this because it is from this age that humans begin to indulge in the works of Venus <note>original: "Veneri." The Roman goddess of love, used here to refer to sexual desire and activity.</note> and to procreate.

### *How they signify Intercourse.*

When signifying "Intercourse" <note>original: "Coitum."</note>, they write the number **16 doubled** <note>This refers to the number 32.</note>. This is because children are the result of pleasures, but intercourse itself arises from two pleasures... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the thought on the next page.</note>

->### *How they signify an ancient lineage.*<-

->### *How they signify Taste.*<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Phoenix bird, which was introduced on the previous page as a symbol for the soul and the inundation of the Nile. It transitions from the Phoenix to symbols for the heart and skill.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

### How they signify him who returns late from a long journey.

When they wish to write about him who returns late from a long journey <note>original: "peregrinatione." While this can mean pilgrimage, in this context it refers to any lengthy travel or time spent abroad.</note>, they again paint the **Phoenix** bird. For after five hundred years, when it is already near death, this bird returns to Egypt. As if anticipating its demise, it is treated with mystical care by the Egyptians, and whatever honors they grant to other sacred animals are all owed to the Phoenix as well. For the Phoenix rejoices most in the Sun, especially in Egypt, where the sun's heat is most intense. Indeed, they say the Nile overflows because of that very heat.

### How they signify the Heart.

When they wish to indicate the "Heart" <note>original: "Cor."</note>, they paint an **Ibis**. This is because this animal is dedicated to Mercury <note>original: "Mercurio." The author identifies the Roman Mercury with the Egyptian god Thoth, who was the patron of wisdom, writing, and the heart.</note>, the lord of all heart and reason. Furthermore, the Ibis itself possesses a heart that is larger than the proportion of its body would suggest—a matter of much discussion among the Egyptians.

### How they signify Skill.

To indicate "Skill" <note>original: "Peritiam," meaning expertise, experience, or practical knowledge.</note>, they paint the **sky raining down dew**. This is because just as the dew falling upon all plants softens those which <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

### How they signify Egyptian letters.

When they wish to represent **Egyptian letters**, or a **sacred scribe**, or an **end**, they paint **ink, a sieve, and a reed**. They paint letters in this way because all things written among the Egyptians are completed with these tools; for they write with a reed <note>original: "iunco." Referring to the calamus or reed pen used for papyrus.</note>, and nothing else. They also add a sieve to the image because this was the first instrument used for making bread, and it too is made from the reed. This indicates that anyone who has a livelihood <note>original: "victum." Meaning the means to support oneself or "daily bread."</note> will devote himself to letters, but he who does not have one will be occupied with a different, manual craft. 

Hence, among them, skill is called **Sbo**, which is interpreted as "full of food." <note>The author is likely referencing the Coptic word "Sbō" (learning or teaching). He provides a folk etymology linking education to the leisure provided by having enough to eat.</note> They use these symbols for a **priest** or a **sacred scribe** because such a person judges life and death. For there is a book kept among the priests called the **Sacred Ambris**, through which they determine whether a sick person is likely to live or die. They use these symbols to signify an **end** as well, because he who...

### *How they again represent a sacred scribe.*

When they wish to signify a **scribe** again, or a **Prophet**, or the **spleen**, or the **sense of smell**, or **laughter**, or **sneezing**, they paint a **dog**. They use it for a **scribe** because he who ought to be a perfect scribe must meditate on many things, and must "bark" at everyone in a certain way; he must be fierce <note>original: "agrestem." Literally "rustic" or "wild," implying a lack of refined flattery or a blunt, honest nature.</note> and gratify no one, just as dogs do not. They use it for a **Prophet** because the dog, more than any other animal, wonders at and gazes with a firm stare at the images of the gods, just like a prophet. They use it for the **spleen** because the dog has a very light spleen, and it is from this organ that death and madness <note>original: "rabies." Referring to both the disease of rabies and a state of fury or agitation.</note> sometimes come to it; furthermore, those attendants who care for a dog when it is about to die usually become splenetic <note>original: "splenetici." In ancient medicine, the spleen was thought to govern certain moods; a splenetic person was considered irritable or melancholy.</note> themselves. They use it for the **sense of smell**, **laughter**, and **sneezing**, because those who are completely splenetic are able neither to smell, nor to laugh, nor to sneeze.

### *How they represent a man who is a magistrate.*

When writing of a **magistrate**, they again paint a **dog**...

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the dog as a symbol for a magistrate, explaining the addition of a royal robe and the relationship between the official and the sovereign. It then moves into symbols for house-guardians, time-keepers, ignorance, and hatred.</meta>

->THE HIEROGLYPHICS OF HORAPOLLO<-

...they paint [a dog], to which they add a **royal robe** <note>original: "regiam stolam." While a *stola* was typically a Roman woman's garment, in this Renaissance Latin translation it refers more broadly to a robe of state or a ceremonial vestment.</note> placed upon a naked figure. This is because, just as a dog (as previously mentioned) gazes with intent eyes upon the statues of the gods, so too did **magistrates** in ancient times gaze upon the naked king, by whose favor the magistrate claims the royal robe for himself.

### *How they represent one who carries a sacred cloak.*

When they wish to signify one who carries a **sacred cloak**, they paint a **guardian of the house**, because the cloak is guarded by him in the same manner as a house is by its guardian.

### *How they represent a horoscoper, that is, one who calculates the hours.*

When they wish to signify a **horoscoper** <term>horoscopus: from the Greek *horoskopos*, meaning "hour-watcher." In ancient Egypt, these were priests who tracked time and celestial movements to schedule rituals.</term>, they paint a **man eating the hours**. This is not because a man actually eats hours—for that is impossible—but because men prepare their food according to the hours.

### *How they represent Ignorance.*

When writing of **Ignorance**, they paint **fire and water**, because through these two elements all **corruption** <note>original: "corruptio." In the context of ancient natural philosophy, "corruption" is the opposite of "generation"; it refers to the process of decay or the destruction of one substance to create another.</note> occurs.

### *How they represent a wicked man, or hatred.*

To signify a **wicked man** and **hatred**, they paint a **fish**, because the use of this...

<meta>This page continues the translation from page 154, completing the entry on why a fish represents a wicked man or hatred before moving into symbols for the mouth, the temperate man, and the sense of hearing.</meta>

->BOOK I.<-

...should be cursed and forbidden in sacred rites; for every fish destroys whatever it finds, nor does it spare even its own kind.

### *How they represent the Mouth.*

To represent the **mouth**, they paint a **snake** <note>original: "Anguẽ" (Anguem). While Egyptian hieroglyphs use various snakes for phonetic sounds and deities, Horapollo interprets the snake's anatomy as a symbol for the mouth's singular power.</note>. For the snake is strong in no other limb except its mouth.

### *How they represent a brave and temperate man.*

When they wish to signify a **brave man** who is also **temperate** <term>temperate: from the Latin *temperatus*, meaning self-controlled or moderate, particularly regarding physical or sexual impulses.</term>, they paint a **bull** in full health. For this animal has a most heated organ, so much so that when joined to a cow, he emits seed without any movement. But if it should ever deviate from its natural place, and direct that organ toward another part of the body, it wounds the cow with its overwhelming intensity. Yet he is also temperate, for after the cow has conceived, he never mates with her again.

### *How they represent Hearing.*

When writing of **hearing**, they paint the **ear of a bull**. For when a cow desires to conceive—and she desires this for no longer than three hours—she then lows as loudly as she can. During this time, if the bull does not run to her, she closes her **womb** <note>original: "naturam." In Renaissance Latin translations of natural history, *natura* is frequently used as a euphemism for the reproductive organs or the biological "nature" of the womb.</note> until another determined time. This, however, rarely happens, for the bull hears her even if he is quite far away:

<meta>catchword: and perceives (& intel-)</meta>
